## [Consistency Is Not Correction: Decomposing Confidence in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21651) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper formally decomposes confidence signals in LLM agents into discrimination, selective prediction, and correction utility, showing that a gate replacing proposals can only correct where the base policy was already wrong, which bounds the possible improvement. Experiments on two model scales confirm the bound binds, with a perturbation-agreement verifier achieving within-policy AUROC 0.990 but no episode outcome improvement at 4.32x the call count. This work reveals that the binding constraint on confidence-based correction in LLM agents is measurement resolution, not the choice of signal, which could prevent wasted research budgets and misleading conclusions. It provides two pre-checks that would catch ineffective experiments before any budget is spent, impacting how practitioners design and evaluate self-correction mechanisms. In a controlled 840-episode study, no verifier replaced the proposal on any of the 60 GSM8K decisions, so that comparison tested nothing; the entire GSM8K gain across six controllers is one task, and it is the task whose identical temperature-zero repeats disagreed. On the diagnostic set the gate could reach four errors while the test needed six, so even a perfect gate had no significant result available.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Selective prediction allows models to abstain when confidence is low, and correction utility measures whether a confidence signal can improve answers by resampling or escalating to a larger model. McNemar's test is a paired statistical test often used to compare classifiers on the same test set, and its power depends on the number of discordant pairs where one model is correct and the other wrong.

## [Agent-driven Semantic Inversion Attack Deceives FinBERT in 90.85% of Cases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21653) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new adversarial attack framework called Agent-driven Semantic Inversion (ASI) uses an LLM-powered multi-agent pipeline to perform targeted lexical substitutions in financial news sentences, successfully deceiving FinBERT in 90.85% of eligible cases. The attack often causes FinBERT to maintain its original sentiment prediction even when financial performance indicators are fundamentally inverted. This finding exposes critical vulnerabilities in FinBERT, a widely deployed financial sentiment model used in automated trading, portfolio management, and risk assessment, threatening the integrity of algorithmic decision-making. It highlights an urgent need for logic-aware, robust financial NLP models to ensure the reliability of automated financial systems. The attack leverages an LLM-powered multi-agent pipeline to identify eligible financial news sentences and apply targeted lexical substitutions, and token-level analysis shows FinBERT over-relies on contextual keywords and syntactic patterns rather than true logical comprehension of financial semantics. The 90.85% success rate is measured only on eligible cases, and the study focuses on FinBERT, so generalizability to other models remains to be tested.

## [Token Commitment as a Control Surface for Steering Masked Diffusion Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21669) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper proposes treating token commitment—the decision of which provisional predictions become permanent—as a diffusion-specific control surface, introducing counterfactual target-aware commitment and calibrated adaptive budgets to steer masked diffusion generation. Experiments across the LLaDA family and Dream-7B show empirically monotonic target-quality trade-offs in every configuration evaluated. This work opens a new inference-time steering axis for masked diffusion models, complementing existing activation, logit, and trajectory modifications without requiring a single reusable steering direction. It could benefit researchers and practitioners working on controllable generation with diffusion language models and vision-language diffusion models. The commitment policy is split into two decisions: which tokens to commit (ranked by a mixture of model confidence and leave-one-token-out target contribution) and how many to commit per step (driven by target progress and confidence). Both mechanisms require only a scalar score on a provisional completion, so they also apply to automatic proxies like NLI entailment for summarization and CLIPScore for captioning.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Masked diffusion models generate text by iteratively predicting tokens for masked positions and then committing some predictions as final, unlike autoregressive models that generate left to right. LLaDA is a family of large language diffusion models trained with a masking objective, and Dream-7B is another masked diffusion model. Inference-time steering typically modifies activations or logits to guide generation toward a target attribute, but commitment policy has largely been treated as a fixed decoding detail.

## [Exact Bounds for Low-Rank Watermark Evidence in Open-Weight LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21702) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper derives exact finite-vocabulary lower bounds on the expected log-likelihood-ratio evidence that an OpenStamp-style watermark verifier can obtain after an open-weight LLM is modified, and provides a sequence-level detection guarantee using a supermartingale argument. Experiments on Llama-2-7B and Mistral-7B-v0.3 show that INT8 quantization and 30% pruning retain true-positive rates of 85.6–94.8%, while 500-step LoRA fine-tuning drops them to 28.8% and 11.6%. This work provides rigorous theoretical guarantees for watermark robustness under model editing, which is crucial for AI security and provenance in open-weight models where users can freely modify weights. It quantifies how different modifications (quantization, pruning, fine-tuning) affect watermark detectability, helping developers and regulators assess risks. The analysis represents modifications as residuals in next-token logits and derives a sharp span-only deviation bound, a first-order evidence shift via a covariance term with explicit second-order remainder, and an exact conditional exponential-moment bound for sequence-level detection. Experiments show zero false positives among 500 base-model continuations per model, and the covariance prediction tracks measured mean LoRA evidence drift with no remainder-bound violations across 160,000 test prefixes.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Open-weight LLMs are models whose weights are publicly released, allowing users to modify them via techniques like quantization, pruning, or low-rank adaptation (LoRA). Watermarking embeds a detectable signal into model outputs to verify provenance, but modifications can weaken this signal. OpenStamp is a watermarking method that modifies the unembedding layer and uses a log-likelihood ratio verifier against a private base model. This paper analyzes how such modifications affect the verifier's evidence.

## [Moral reasoning in LLMs relies on general normative evaluation circuits, not moral-specific features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21705) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.21705) uses 227 annotator-validated minimal pairs across 14 open-weight models (Llama, Gemma, Qwen, 1B–14B) to show that attention heads driving moral answers are part of a general normative-evaluation circuit, not specific to moral content or the word 'morally'. This challenges the assumption that models have dedicated moral circuits, suggesting that moral judgments are handled by domain-general evaluation mechanisms, which has implications for AI safety and interpretability efforts aimed at aligning model values. The heads carry the same share of effect for moral and other normative pairs (pooled ratio 1.00 with the word, 0.90 without), and removing them on ETHICS scenarios reduces the acceptable-unacceptable difference by 37–39% versus 1–3% for random heads; SAE features mostly respond to the word 'morally' rather than content, and the category of a pair can be read from hidden states.

## [EJBench: A New Benchmark for Engineering Judgement in Heat Transfer Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21707) ⭐️ 7.0/10

EJBench introduces a software-agnostic benchmark for engineering judgement derived from a conjugate heat transfer design campaign for battery cold plates, with tasks such as matching unlabelled test results to operating points and ranking new duties by peak cell temperature. The best of ten frontier models, GPT-6 Astra, scores only 0.71, and models show poor calibration, stating almost the same confidence on wrong answers as on right ones. This benchmark shifts evaluation from tool operation to engineering judgement—what an engineer would conclude without running a simulation—and grounds answers in converged simulations rather than LLM judges, providing a more reliable measure of AI's readiness for real design work. The finding that frontier models are poorly calibrated has direct implications for deploying AI in high-stakes engineering decisions. The benchmark includes three task families and releases train/validation/test splits, an evaluation harness, and a leaderboard. The authors also post-train Qwen3.5-9B with LoRA and reinforcement learning rewarded by simulation-confirmed answer criteria, improving its held-out score from 0.071 to 0.106, though absolute performance remains low.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Conjugate heat transfer is the coupled analysis of heat conduction in a solid and convection in an adjacent fluid, with continuous temperature and heat flux at the interface. Battery cold plates are liquid-cooled plates used in EV battery packs to manage thermal load. EJBench tests whether a model can infer design behaviour at new conditions from a few prior simulation results, mimicking an engineer's judgement rather than software operation.

## [LLMs as Validators for Solana Cross-Chain Bridges: A Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21777) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper evaluates five open-weight LLMs across six configurations on their ability to detect source-level and transaction-level vulnerabilities in a Solana cross-chain bridge, using paired vulnerable and secure programs and 16,800 responses. The study finds that without comments, models correctly diagnose only 24% of vulnerable source inputs while frequently flagging the secure bridge, and transaction diagnosis averages 6% across three of four scenarios. Cross-chain bridges have suffered massive losses from exploits, and this study shows that current LLMs are far from reliable as automated validators, often raising unsupported alarms rather than providing correct diagnoses. This matters for blockchain security teams considering LLM-assisted audits and highlights the need for better evaluation methods that distinguish true diagnoses from false positives. The study uses a native Rust lock-and-mint bridge on a local Solana validator with four exploitable variants, including two modeled after the Cashio and Wormhole incidents, and grades 7,200 answers using two blinded LLM graders. Original comments, some describing the flaw, increase source diagnosis accuracy to 60%, showing that LLM performance is highly sensitive to code comments and context.

LLMs are increasingly explored as tools for code and transaction analysis, but their ability to diagnose specific vulnerabilities rather than just flag anomalies is not well understood.

## [TriX-EEG: A Three-Level Framework for Trustworthy EEG Artifact Removal Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21781) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces TriX-EEG, a unified three-level framework for evaluating EEG artifact removal models that assesses signal validity, removal faithfulness, and explanation reliability, moving beyond reconstruction-only metrics. It evaluates nine published neural denoisers across three benchmarks covering EOG, EMG, and motion artifacts, and validates on independent EEG/EOG and BCI Competition IV-2a datasets. Current evaluation of EEG denoisers relies heavily on reconstruction metrics like error and correlation, which do not guarantee that artifacts are truly removed or neural activity preserved. TriX-EEG provides a more comprehensive assessment that could drive the development of denoisers that are not only accurate but also faithful and explainable, impacting EEG/BCI research and clinical applications. The framework's three levels are: Signal Validity (reconstruction quality and neural band-power preservation), Removal Faithfulness (artifact error and residual artifact/neural leakage), and Explanation Reliability (temporal alignment, causal relevance, and stability). Results show that reconstruction metrics alone are insufficient, and higher-ranked denoisers generally preserve more task-relevant information or cause less downstream performance degradation.

**Background**: EEG (electroencephalography) records the brain's electrical activity and is widely used in neuroscience and brain-computer interfaces (BCIs). Deep learning models have been developed to remove these artifacts, but their evaluation has primarily focused on how closely the cleaned signal matches a clean reference, rather than whether the artifacts are truly removed and neural signals preserved.

A new arXiv paper revisits the survival of agent-attributed code using a specification curve of 88 survival analyses across nine measurement choices, finding that for fully-autonomous agent Devin the verdict flips depending on choices like clustering standard errors by repository, while for human-supervised agents such as GitHub Copilot the effect size changes but direction does not. This work shows that conflicting conclusions in empirical software engineering about AI-generated code may stem from arbitrary measurement choices rather than real differences, urging researchers to adopt more robust and transparent methodologies. The study analyzes 854,139 lines from 2,802 merged PRs in 215 repositories, and finds that LLM intent classifiers agree with human labels as well as two humans agree with each other (kappa = 0.69–0.72), whereas keyword rules do not (kappa = 0.38); it also provides an operationalization checklist for post-merge studies.

## [Coding Agents Excel at Test Coverage but Struggle with Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21797) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.21797) benchmarks three popular coding agents, each using a native frontier model with high reasoning effort, on writing and translating unit tests. It finds that agents far exceed human-written test suites on traditional coverage metrics, but their advantage is uneven: quality degrades sharply with project size and much of the gain comes from writing more tests rather than better ones. As LLM-based coding assistants become standard in software development, trust in their output increasingly relies on tests they write themselves, so understanding their limitations in test generation and translation is critical for safe adoption. The findings warn that agentic test translation, while seemingly strong in aggregate, remains inconsistent and unreliable, which could introduce subtle bugs when modernizing or porting code across languages. The study evaluates three coding agents, each configured with a native frontier model and high reasoning effort, across a broad suite of metrics. It reveals that the coverage advantage is largely due to a greater number of written tests rather than superior test quality, and that agentic test translation is inconsistent despite appearing strong in aggregate.

## [Inventa: Decentralized Self-Improving LLM Agents for Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21847) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Inventa introduces a decentralized framework of autonomous research units that use mutator agents to generate candidate ideas, local orchestrators to adapt search strategies, and verifiers to record only verified improvements in a shared knowledge base. It outperforms baselines on five benchmarks, achieving 73.6% on EurekaBench versus 56.2% for CORAL and discovering 26.0 novel ideas per paper on IdeaBench compared to 18.6 for ResearchAgent with 82% fewer model calls. This work addresses key limitations in LLM-based scientific discovery agents—convergence on similar ideas, propagation of unsupported claims, and stale search strategies—by introducing verified shared knowledge and adaptive orchestration. It demonstrates that decentralized, self-improving agent architectures can yield more diverse and feasible research ideas with greater efficiency, potentially accelerating AI-driven scientific innovation. The framework uses a mutator agent to create a lineage of candidate ideas, a local orchestrator that periodically adapts the mutator's harness and replaces its model or restarts evolution when progress stalls, and verifiers that record verified improvements, useful components, and constraint violations in a shared knowledge base—the only channel between units. Evaluation spans retrieval (ScholarCatalyst), ideation (IdeaBench), proposal writing (IdeaScientist), and scientific law discovery (EurekaBench, SciLaws-Bench).

**Background**: LLM agents are increasingly used to automate parts of the scientific discovery process, such as generating hypotheses or research proposals. Inventa tackles these issues by structuring agents into autonomous units with verified knowledge sharing and adaptive orchestration, drawing on trends in self-improving LLM agents and multi-agent systems for science.

AR-WAM introduces a streaming world-action model that uses a causal video backbone to incrementally update visual context and a single forward pass to obtain future features for conditioning trajectory generation. After supervised training it achieves 91.5 PDMS on NAVSIM-v1, and reinforcement learning further raises performance to 94.0 PDMS, outperforming strong end-to-end world-action baselines. This work addresses the efficiency bottleneck of video foundation models in autonomous driving by eliminating repeated history encoding and iterative video generation, enabling real-time streaming inference. The combination of incremental computation and reinforcement learning for direct driving reward optimization could make world-action models more practical for deployment in dynamic traffic scenarios. AR-WAM uses only a single front camera and maintains visual history across decisions in continuous driving settings, where supervised AR-WAM outperforms strong world-action baselines. The method relies on a causal video backbone to reuse past computation and conditions future prediction on observed motion without iterative video generation.

AR-WAM builds on these ideas by combining a causal video backbone with single-pass future feature conditioning and reinforcement learning to optimize driving rewards.

## [MoE Diffusion Decoding Bottleneck: Logits Pipeline, Not Expert GEMMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21852) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Profiling a 26B-parameter diffusion Mixture-of-Experts language model on an NVIDIA GB10 edge accelerator reveals that the per-token sampling and stopping logits pipeline, not the expert GEMMs, dominates decode time after weight quantization. The authors propose two lossless optimization tiers—a bit-exact tier and an accuracy-parity tier with a fused single-pass entropy kernel and Gumbel-max sampling—achieving 2.4–2.7× end-to-end speedup over bf16 with unchanged accuracy on GSM8K, HumanEval, and MATH. This work highlights a non-obvious performance bottleneck specific to MoE diffusion models on bandwidth-bound edge hardware, showing that optimizing the logits sampling pipeline can yield significant speedups without accuracy loss. It provides practical insights for deploying large diffusion language models on edge devices and releases code and quantized weights. The speedup is achieved by combining the optimized logits pipeline with a W4A16 expert kernel on a single 273 GB/s device. The finding is confirmed on a second independently built diffusion MoE (LLaDA2.0-mini: pipeline 28.8% of decode, fused W4A16 3.3× at accuracy parity) but disappears on a dense diffusion model (3.6%), indicating the cost is specific to MoE diffusion where quantization makes the backbone cheap.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Masked diffusion language models (dLLMs) generate text by iteratively denoising a full canvas of tokens, unlike autoregressive models that generate one token at a time. This means each forward pass processes logits for all positions across multiple denoising steps, leading to S times more logit entries per output token. Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models activate only a subset of parameters per token, reducing computation but introducing expert GEMMs. On bandwidth-bound edge accelerators like NVIDIA GB10, memory bandwidth is the limiting factor, making the logits pipeline a significant cost.

## [PromptPack: Training-Free Spatial and Semantic Control for Diffusion Editors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21865) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PromptPack introduces a training-free method that augments diffusion-based image editors with explicit spatial and semantic control through two 'Edit Guides': an edit-role partition labeling each pixel as 'Keep,' 'Permit,' or 'Modify,' and a regional-prompt assignment mapping spatial regions to text prompts. It was integrated into FlowEdit FLUX, FlowEdit SD3, and FLUX Kontext, showing improved concept placement and content preservation on PIE-Bench++ and an 87% user preference over FlowEdit FLUX. This work addresses a key limitation in instruction-driven and inpainting-based diffusion editors, which often leave user intent underspecified regarding where edits occur and which concepts apply where. By providing a training-free interface for precise spatial and semantic control, PromptPack could significantly improve practical editing workflows without requiring model retraining. PromptPack injects independently embedded regional prompts into the editor's fixed-length text sequence and uses the Edit Guides to spatially mask attention, gate flow updates, and restore protected source pixels. The guides can be manually specified or automatically derived from a text-grounded SAM 3 mask for source-object edits, and the method was evaluated on PIE-Bench++ across three integrations.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Diffusion models have become a dominant approach for image generation and editing, with methods like inpainting allowing users to modify specific regions while preserving the rest. However, instruction-driven editors often lack precise control over where edits are applied and which concepts affect which areas. PromptPack builds on this by introducing explicit spatial and semantic guides, leveraging techniques like SAM (Segment Anything Model) for automatic mask generation.

## [FaceRiff: Data-Efficient Text-to-3D Face Asset Synthesis via Geometric Conditioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21867) ⭐️ 7.0/10

FaceRiff introduces a unified framework that generates complete 3D face assets (mesh, albedo, and displacement) from text by adapting a pretrained SDXL backbone with task-specific LoRA adapters and using UV normal maps as geometric conditioning. Trained on only 2,049 scans from about 350 identities on a single GPU, it updates less than 3% of backbone parameters per task and produces render-ready assets without per-instance optimization. This work addresses data scarcity and computational efficiency in text-to-3D face generation, enabling high-quality, diverse, and relightable 3D face assets from text, which could significantly impact digital-human rendering, gaming, and virtual production. It demonstrates that pretrained image diffusion priors can be effectively adapted to unified 3D asset synthesis under limited data. FaceRiff uses per-instance normals to guide albedo and displacement synthesis, while a dataset-mean normal provides a canonical spatial anchor for mesh generation, ensuring fixed-topology UV consistency. A blind study with 45 industry professionals shows it achieves substantially higher perceived realism than DreamFace, approaching that of real captures, and it uniquely generates lighting-independent albedo for PBR relighting and pore-scale displacement.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Text-to-3D generation aims to create 3D models from text descriptions, but faces are challenging due to the need for high-resolution details and consistent UV mapping. SDXL is a powerful text-to-image diffusion model that encodes strong facial priors, and LoRA is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique that adapts large models by adding small trainable matrices. UV mapping is a process that projects a 2D texture onto a 3D surface, and normal maps encode surface orientation details for realistic rendering.

## [Domain-Tuned Diffusion Upscaler Achieves 4x Super-Resolution for Facial Asset Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21890) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper presents a domain-tuned diffusion upscaler that uses LoRA-adapted SDXL and Tile ControlNet to achieve 4x super-resolution (1024 to 4096) of facial albedo and displacement maps. On held-out maps, the method reduces albedo LPIPS from 0.21 to 0.08 and avoids misplaced or excessive displacement relief compared to bilinear and off-the-shelf GAN/diffusion super-resolvers. This work shows that lightweight domain adaptation of a general-purpose diffusion model can produce render-ready 4K facial asset maps without designing a custom super-resolution architecture, potentially streamlining asset creation for cinematic close-ups in visual effects and game production. It also demonstrates the value of tailoring diffusion models to domain-specific statistics, which could inspire similar adaptations for other texture types. The method starts from bilinearly upsampled maps, freezes the SDXL backbone, and trains lightweight LoRA modules per modality (albedo and displacement), while a Tile ControlNet preserves input structure as the diffusion prior restores pore- and wrinkle-scale detail. The approach is evaluated on held-out maps and compared against bilinear, GAN-based, and diffusion-based super-resolvers, with LPIPS used as the primary fidelity metric.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Physically based facial assets for film and games require high-resolution albedo (color) and displacement (height) maps to capture fine skin detail in close-ups, but generative pipelines often output only 1K maps. Super-resolution aims to upscale these maps, but photographic methods are suboptimal because facial UV maps have unique statistics and displacement errors directly distort geometry. Diffusion models like Stable Diffusion XL (SDXL) are powerful generative models that can be adapted to specific domains via techniques such as LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation), which freezes the base model and injects small trainable matrices, and ControlNet, which adds spatial conditioning to guide generation.

## [Counterfactual Test Reveals Models Often Ignore Cited Evidence in Reasoning Chains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21940) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces a counterfactual masking test called the 'cite gap' to measure whether video-language models actually rely on the regions they cite in their reasoning chains. Applied to 2-3B parameter models, the cite gap is 0.089 on CLEVRER but only 0.003 on NExT-QA, showing that cited boxes can pass grounding checks without being causally necessary for the answer. This work exposes a critical flaw in current evaluation of grounded reasoning: verifiable citations do not guarantee that the model's answer depends on the cited evidence. It offers an annotation-free metric that could become a standard for trustworthy multimodal interpretability, affecting how researchers validate chain-of-thought grounding in video-language models. The method blacks out cited regions and compares answer flips against control boxes of the same size elsewhere on the same frames; masking cited boxes flips 2.2 times as many correct answers on CLEVRER but only 1.1 times on NExT-QA. The near-zero gap on NExT-QA is predicted by its supervision, which trains chains to cite two frames of entities that remain visible on most frames, yet standard grounding checks fail to detect this.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Grounded reasoning chains in video-language models provide step-by-step justifications that cite specific frame spans and bounding boxes, allowing humans to check where the model looked. CLEVRER is a synthetic video dataset for causal and temporal reasoning about colliding objects, while NExT-QA is a real-world video question answering benchmark focused on causal and temporal actions in daily activities. The paper asks whether these citations are merely plausible or actually causally used by the model.

## [ThermLeT: Transformer-based Temperature Prediction for 2.5D Chiplet Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21954) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced ThermLeT, a multimodal transformer-based surrogate model that predicts steady-state temperatures in 2.5D chiplet packages in 13 ms, about four orders of magnitude faster than ANSYS finite element solvers. Trained on a new dataset of 6,221 chiplet layouts, it achieves an RMSE of 3.16°C and MAE of 2.24°C on a 150-sample test set, improving accuracy by 35% over ThermEDGe and 71% over HotSpot while running 6x and 11x faster, respectively. This work enables rapid thermal-aware design space exploration for heterogeneous 2.5D chiplet packages, where high density amplifies thermal challenges. By providing orders-of-magnitude speedup with improved accuracy over existing tools, it could significantly accelerate EDA workflows and help designers avoid thermal hotspots early in the design cycle. ThermLeT integrates power density maps, spatial coordinate embeddings, material properties, and heat transfer rate as inputs, and is scale-invariant. Ablation studies confirm robust generalization to unseen floorplans, and the code and dataset are publicly available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: 2.5D chiplet architectures integrate multiple smaller dies (chiplets) on an interposer, offering performance and customization benefits but creating dense thermal hotspots. Traditional thermal analysis relies on finite element solvers like ANSYS, which are accurate but computationally expensive, or fast architectural simulators like HotSpot, which trade accuracy for speed. Surrogate modeling uses machine learning to approximate expensive simulations, and transformers are a neural network architecture well-suited for capturing complex spatial relationships.

## [Occam: MLP framework improves single-cell representation and perturbation prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21959) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced Occam, a multilayer perceptron (MLP) framework that combines denoising-based cell representations with gene interaction, text, and protein-sequence features. It achieves the highest aggregate representation scores for perturbation information and cell-type preservation with batch integration, and outperforms four retrained competitors in cross-context few-shot perturbation prediction. This work shows that simple MLPs can effectively generalize to unseen perturbation-context combinations, which is crucial for predicting genetic perturbation effects in new cell types and gene combinations. It could impact computational biology research by providing a lightweight alternative to more complex models for single-cell analysis and perturbation prediction. Occam better captures measured response patterns for unobserved perturbation-context pairs than direct transfer from other cell lines, and without fine-tuning it best distinguishes perturbations in held-out cell lines across seen and unseen assays. For double perturbations, it composes learned single-gene responses to reduce expression error, though accurately predicting expression levels remains challenging.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Single-cell RNA sequencing measures gene expression at the individual cell level, but the data is noisy and high-dimensional, so representation learning methods like denoising autoencoders are used to extract meaningful features. Predicting the effects of genetic perturbations (e.g., CRISPR knockouts) is important for understanding gene function and disease, but models often struggle to generalize to new cell contexts or gene combinations. Few-shot learning aims to make predictions for unseen combinations given limited training data, and MLPs are a basic neural network architecture known for simplicity and efficiency.

Researchers propose PathoDraw, a generative model that synthesizes brain MRI with pathology by drawing lesions into label maps before contrast-agnostic rendering, and trains a single contrast-agnostic 3D UNet to estimate healthy anatomy and signed intensity deviation. The model achieves zero-shot pathology detection on public stroke, tumor, and white matter hyperintensity datasets across T1w, FLAIR, and T2w at various resolutions, without training on real pathology. This approach could unlock uncurated hospital archives for large-scale clinical analysis, especially for underrepresented populations and diseases, by eliminating the need for contrast-specific models or real annotated pathology data. It enables robust abnormality detection across acquisition settings and disease types, potentially scaling neuroimaging research and diagnosis. The contrast-agnostic 3D UNet learns healthy labels under lesions and signed deviation directly, identifying pathology via robust tissue statistics and learned features despite no real pathology training. The single model exceeds or performs comparably to contrast-specific anomaly detectors trained on real scans, and the same generator can train networks for super-resolution and registration in the presence of pathology.

Synthetic data has enabled contrast-agnostic models for healthy anatomy, but pathology detection in clinical settings has remained challenging. PathoDraw addresses this by generating synthetic pathology priors to train a model that can detect lesions without ever seeing real pathology.

Researchers introduced DiagTrace, a source-grounded benchmark built from PubMed Central case reports to evaluate whether LLMs can incrementally seek diagnostic evidence and reconstruct clinical courses. The benchmark contains 510 cases across eight specialties, with 225 used to evaluate four LLMs on clinical-course alignment, interaction quality, and efficiency. Existing medical benchmarks typically provide all patient information upfront and assess only the final diagnosis, ignoring the real-world need to acquire evidence incrementally from incomplete information. DiagTrace addresses this gap by evaluating interactive diagnostic reasoning, which could lead to more realistic assessment of LLMs for clinical decision support. Each benchmark instance includes an initial presentation, an event bank, a clinical course, and diagnostic references, and results reveal trade-offs in coverage, source-consistent ordering, and turn use. The abstract is truncated, so full details on the evaluation metrics and model performance are not available.

## [CPTC: Near-Optimal Quantization with Two Multiplications and a 64-Byte Table](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22019) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose Computed Permutation Trellis Coding (CPTC), a low-bit tensor quantization format whose decoder multiplies its state by two coefficients and adds the top bits of the products to index a 64-byte table. CPTC achieves within 0.07 bits of the information-theoretic ideal at 4 bits, matches a random-permutation trellis code with a table thousands of times larger, and has been built into the AWS Trainium 4 AI accelerator. This technique significantly improves low-bit quantization quality for large language models without fine-tuning, reducing KL divergence by up to 27% compared to QTIP codebooks on Llama 3.1 models. Its negligible area overhead and native-speed decoding on Trainium 4 make it practical for real-world deployment. The decoder uses a non-uniform sum to index a small table, allowing rare table entries to represent tail values, and one decoder serves all bit widths from 1 to 6 while exactly reproducing standard integer and floating-point formats. Fused encoding kernels quantize an 8B model's linear layers in under two minutes on one GPU and in cycle-level simulation on one Trainium 4 chip.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Low-bit quantization reduces the memory and compute footprint of neural networks by storing tensors at lower precision, but formats like INT4 and FP4 round each value independently and waste bits relative to the information-theoretic ideal. Trellis coded quantization (TCQ) improves efficiency by encoding values jointly using a trellis diagram, but prior decoders either require large lookup tables or use restrictive hashing. CPTC builds on permutation trellis codes, which were originally developed for communication systems, to enable efficient joint decoding with a tiny computed table.

## [GauntletScore matches top fact-checkers on LLM-AggreFact with auditable evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22027) ⭐️ 7.0/10

GauntletScore, an evidence engine that combines independent readers via a fixed Bayesian rule, achieves 79.51 balanced accuracy on the full LLM-AggreFact test split (29,320 claims across 11 datasets), surpassing every average published on the leaderboard as of 15 November 2026 and matching the best published checker, nimble-v4-max (79.45). It also records the evidence behind each verdict, unlike single-model checkers. This work shows that an ensemble of off-the-shelf readers with a transparent aggregation rule can match state-of-the-art fact-checking performance while providing auditable evidence, which is valuable for AI safety and regulated applications where traceability is required. It also demonstrates strong gains on full-length news articles checked against multi-sentence summaries, a format common in regulated writing. The engine's 95% confidence interval (78.47–80.53) contains the top published score, and it beats two of its three constituent readers by 1.66 and 2.17 points; even without the AggreFact-CNN dataset, the leads remain positive (0.90 and 1.32 points). The test split was run once on a frozen engine under a pre-committed stopping plan, and per-claim predictions and scoring script are released.

**Background**: LLM-AggreFact is a benchmark that aggregates 11 public datasets for grounded factuality evaluation, measuring whether a generated sentence is supported by its source document. Balanced accuracy is a metric that averages recall across classes, useful for imbalanced datasets. GauntletScore uses typed questions posed to independent readers from different providers, records answers in an evidence ledger, and applies a Bayesian rule that counts a contradiction only when two readers agree.

## [FM-Mamba: A Native 2D State Space Model with Stability-Aware Discretization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22061) ⭐️ 7.0/10

FM-Mamba introduces a native 2D selective state space model based on the Fornasini-Marchesini framework, featuring coupled recurrences along both spatial axes, input-dependent step sizes, and a stability-aware discretization that projects step sizes onto a closed-form bound when configurations fall outside the stable interval. This work addresses key limitations of existing Mamba-based vision models by avoiding flattening of feature maps and enforcing 2D stability, potentially enabling more efficient and spatially coherent processing for image tasks and advancing the adoption of state space models in computer vision. The model uses a normalized ratio r_k = a_{0,k}/(a_{1,k}a_{2,k}) to derive a piecewise bound on the two step sizes, and the recurrence is executed via a dual-diagonal wavefront scan that gathers information from two anti-diagonals without flattening the feature map during training or inference; code is available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: State space models (SSMs) like Mamba have gained popularity as efficient alternatives to Transformers for sequence modeling. The Fornasini-Marchesini model is a classic 2D state-space representation that naturally extends recurrence to two dimensions, making it suitable for image data. However, adapting Mamba to 2D images often involves flattening or simplified recurrences that ignore 2D stability theory, which FM-Mamba aims to overcome.

## [HyCoInfer: Cloud-Edge LLM Inference on Heterogeneous GPU-FPGA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22086) ⭐️ 7.0/10

HyCoInfer proposes a heterogeneous cloud-edge serving system that offloads LLM prefill to cloud GPUs and distributes decoding across cloud GPUs and edge FPGAs. Implemented on two NVIDIA V100 GPUs and an AMD Xilinx Alveo U280, it achieves up to 4.11x the request goodput of cloud-only vLLM and reduces mean end-to-end latency by up to 61.1% at 500 Mbps. This work addresses the memory-bound nature of autoregressive decoding, which leaves cloud GPU compute underutilized, by leveraging edge FPGAs for efficient decoding. It demonstrates a practical path to improving LLM serving capacity and energy efficiency without solely relying on expensive cloud GPUs. HyCoInfer tackles three barriers: capacity balancing across heterogeneous accelerators, KV cache transfer over constrained WAN links, and added latency from heterogeneous execution. It combines layer-wise pipelined transfer, decoder-side precomputation, and bandwidth-adaptive KV cache compression based on residual vector quantization, with gains evaluated over 100-1000 Mbps links.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: LLM inference consists of two phases: prefill, which processes the input prompt in parallel and is compute-intensive, and decoding, which generates tokens one at a time and is memory-bound. The KV cache stores key-value states of previously generated tokens to avoid recomputation, but it can be large and must be transferred when offloading decoding to edge devices. FPGAs offer energy-efficient acceleration for memory-bound workloads but are challenging to program and scale for high-concurrency serving.

## [Analog RF Computing Moves Semantic Encoder into Edge Receiver](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22100) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper proposes an end-to-end semantic communication framework that executes the semantic encoder in an edge device's analog RF front end using passive mixers, with the base station broadcasting encoder weights as RF waveforms. The work introduces a unified noise model for analog computing and channel noise, a task-posterior semantic distortion metric, and a closed-form semantic-aware water-filling allocation that reduces distortion by 9.2%-19.2% over uniform precision in MNIST simulations. This approach could dramatically lower the energy and memory cost of running semantic encoders on resource-constrained edge devices, making semantic communication more practical for low-power IoT and edge AI deployments. It also bridges analog computing and wireless communications, suggesting a new co-design direction for future networks. The framework reuses the receiver's passive mixer for analog matrix-vector multiplication, and the allocation problem accounts for client-specific, per-layer accuracy requirements, inducing a broadcast beamforming problem. Simulations are limited to MNIST, and noise-aware co-design yields a further 46% distortion reduction, but real-world hardware validation remains future work.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Semantic communication is an emerging paradigm that transmits the meaning of data rather than raw bits, using deep learning to extract compact latent representations. Analog computing performs computation directly in the analog domain using physical phenomena, potentially offering ultra-low energy consumption for operations like matrix-vector multiplication. Passive mixers are common components in RF receivers that can perform frequency conversion with minimal power, and reusing them for computation is a novel idea.

## [Deep RL for HVAC control: small gains over tuned rules in four Modelica case studies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22144) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper presents four small, reproducible Modelica case studies of HVAC supervisory control, training soft actor-critic agents directly on exported Functional Mock-up Units (FMUs) and comparing them against tuned rule-based controllers and near-optimal references. In the two cases without storage, the agent reached near-optimal performance but beat a well-chosen rule by only 1.3% and 0.18%, while in a pre-cooling case a hand-tuned rule outperformed the agent (-12.0% vs -5.6%). This work addresses a key question in applied deep reinforcement learning for HVAC: when does DRL actually provide meaningful gains over well-tuned conventional controls? By using reproducible case studies and near-optimal references, it highlights that reported DRL benefits are often small and that studies should report the margin between the best rule and a near-optimal reference, which is valuable for practitioners and researchers evaluating DRL for building automation. The agents were trained with soft actor-critic directly on co-simulation FMUs, and the paper reports practical difficulties encountered in this training setup. In the chilled water storage case, the agent achieved a 7.8% saving (vs 3.9% and 6.3% for rules) and adopted midday charging during the solar off-peak period, but only obtained about 45% of the saving from a perfect-foresight schedule and did little to limit the demand peak.

**Background**: HVAC supervisory control determines setpoints and operating schedules for building heating, ventilation, and air-conditioning systems. Deep reinforcement learning (DRL) has been proposed to optimize such control, but reported gains are hard to interpret because baselines are often weak and the attainable optimum is rarely known. Modelica is an open-source modeling language, and the Modelica Buildings library provides dynamic simulation models for building energy systems; FMUs (Functional Mock-up Units) are standardized containers for exchanging simulation models, allowing the trained agent to interact with the plant model directly.

## [CueClash Reveals VLMs Rely on 2-D Cues for 3-D Judgments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22180) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces CueClash, a diagnostic benchmark that separates 2-D picture cues from 3-D ground layout by rendering scenes where the 2-D cue for each of five spatial relations is correct on exactly half the statements. Testing frontier VLMs including Claude Opus 5.5, GPT-5.5, and Gemini 3.6 Flash, the authors find a shortcut gap of +18 to +37 percentage points when the 2-D cue is right versus wrong, even though the models answer 99-100% correctly when given ground coordinates and relation definitions. The results show that frontier VLMs often take a shortcut by relying on 2-D picture-plane cues rather than reconstructing true 3-D layout, which could undermine their reliability in embodied AI, robotics, and any application requiring accurate spatial understanding. The finding that the failure lies specifically in the picture-to-layout step provides an actionable target for improving VLM evaluation and training. The shortcut gap drops to +7 to +29 percentage points when excluding two relations whose cue-wrong items are confounded with borderline cases or occlusion, and pixel measurements with camera depths remove most of the gap for objects fitting through openings. The models' top-down maps mostly preserve depth order but shorten depth like the picture, with GPT-5.5's maps staying close to a copy of the picture while Opus 5.5's and Gemini's undo about 0.6 of this shortening.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) are AI systems that process both images and text, and they are increasingly used for tasks requiring spatial reasoning. In photographs, the 2-D arrangement of objects on the picture plane usually aligns with their 3-D layout in the scene, so models can exploit this correlation as a shortcut. CueClash breaks this correlation by rendering scenes where the 2-D cue is deliberately misleading half the time, allowing researchers to measure reliance on the shortcut.

## [EchoMoE Compresses Activation Communication for Efficient Expert-Parallel MoE Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22215) ⭐️ 7.0/10

EchoMoE introduces a lightweight architecture modification that compresses activation communication in expert-parallel MoE inference by reusing earlier residual-stream hidden states as references and transmitting only compressed differences. It reduces inter-node communication volume by 58% and improves throughput by 22.6%, while preserving average performance within 3.8 percentage points across 18 benchmarks, and requires approximately 8,500x fewer training tokens than full pretraining. As compute throughput outpaces interconnect bandwidth, synchronous collective communication has become a major bottleneck for scaling MoE models, especially in bandwidth-constrained environments like Ethernet. EchoMoE's retrofit approach enables more efficient distributed inference and paves the way for wide-area distributed inference without significant retraining costs. EchoMoE periodically refreshes reference states and transmits only compressed differences synchronously, reducing traffic on latency-critical paths without significant computational overhead. It also tolerates quantization of the communicated representations without measurable quality loss, and an efficient implementation for pretrained MoE models with vLLM integration is released.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models scale parameter capacity without proportional compute increase by activating only a subset of experts per token. Expert parallelism distributes experts across devices, requiring two synchronous collective communication steps per MoE layer: dispatching hidden states to activated experts and combining their outputs. As models grow, these all-to-all exchanges become a bottleneck because interconnect bandwidth lags behind compute throughput. The residual stream in transformers is updated incrementally across layers, meaning hidden states change gradually, which EchoMoE exploits by communicating only differences from earlier states.

## [ToR-OT: Autoregressive Transformers Learn Optimal Transport Plans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22218) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce ToR-OT, a transformer-based model that reframes discrete optimal transport (OT) as a sequence generation task and predicts sparse transport plans via autoregressive token regression. Pretrained on a large synthetic corpus, a single model generalizes zero-shot to unseen distribution pairs and heterogeneous cost metrics without retraining. This work shows that autoregressive transformers can internalize the structure of constrained optimization problems like OT, enabling amortized inference that avoids solving an optimization problem at test time. It could benefit machine learning and optimization researchers by providing fast approximate OT plans for low-dimensional discrete problems, though it is not yet a replacement for classical solvers. ToR-OT exploits the inherent sparsity of exact discrete OT solutions by decoding only nonzero entries, avoiding dense coupling outputs. On low-dimensional discrete problems, it gives accurate approximate plans and compares favorably with per-instance neural OT baselines, but the authors note it is not yet a replacement for classical solvers.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Optimal transport is a mathematical framework for finding the minimum-cost way to move mass between two probability distributions, with classical solvers remaining the gold standard. Amortized optimization aims to learn a model that directly predicts solutions to a family of optimization problems, avoiding repeated solving at test time. Autoregressive models, common in language modeling, generate sequences one token at a time, and here they are adapted to output sparse transport plans as token sequences.

A new arXiv paper (2611.22239) proposes a solver-free online optimization rule inspired by dual decomposition that assigns each arriving AI task a start period to minimize energy cost under operational constraints. Validated on an A100 GPU trace with 3,456 GPUs and 1,343 tasks, it achieves lower cost and much less computation time than a multi-timescale optimization benchmark. This addresses a real timing gap between continuous task arrival and per-period electricity pricing, enabling data centers to shift flexible workloads to low-price periods without the latency of running a solver. It could significantly reduce energy costs and improve operational efficiency for large-scale AI data centers. The rule keeps GPU and CPU use within capacity while shifting flexible tasks to low-price periods and keeping high-priority tasks on schedule. The validation uses a real A100 GPU trace, but the approach is heuristic and may not guarantee optimality compared to full optimization.

## [OfficePrism benchmark evaluates AI agents on 420 enterprise tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22262) ⭐️ 7.0/10

OfficePrism introduces a benchmark of 420 single-domain enterprise tasks across seven business functions, evaluated under shared L1–L3 task-structure levels. Across 124 model–harness–package configurations, the joint natural-language-and-database pass rate ranges from 22.6% to 76.4%, with a median of 67.9%. This benchmark addresses a gap in agent evaluation by isolating competence within a single business function, making failures easier to localize than broad workplace scores that mix cross-functional coordination costs. It provides a localized measure of enterprise automation readiness, relevant to researchers and practitioners building AI agents for workplace operations. Each task runs in a fresh environment combining a shared synthetic organization with one function-specific domain and typed state access, and is constructed from grounded records, business rules, and tool schemas to produce a Chinese-language ticket, a hidden executable reference witness, and an expected response. Results show L1 is highest in all 124 configurations and L2 exceeds L3 in 122, while among 50 configurations sharing the same harness and model labels, the search-and-dispatch package scores higher in 30, ties in one, and lower in 19.

A new arXiv paper (2611.22270) distills 38,397 Japan Patent Office search reports into executable expert strategies and trains language models via two offline reinforcement learning approaches: strategy-level learning and line-level citation-preserving narrowing credit. The line-level method reduces the median citation-containing result set from 768 to under 50 documents, outperforming strategy-level scores on screening measures. Patent prior-art search is a critical but labor-intensive task where Boolean queries often return thousands of documents that must be manually screened. This work shows that language models can learn to generate much narrower, high-precision queries from expert feedback, potentially reducing screening burden and improving efficiency in patent examination and litigation. The line-level credit upweights query lines that keep a found citation in a smaller set, and iterated training places more citations in sets under 100 documents than experts, though at lower recall. Strategy-level preference learning achieves near-complete recall, and the two forms are complementary.

## [Popcornn: Neural Network Path Optimization for Transition States on MLIPs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22272) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce Popcornn, a framework that finds transition states by optimizing continuous neural network paths on a machine-learned interatomic potential (MLIP) using a parallelized adaptive numerical integrator. Across thousands of organic reactions and multiple MLIPs, Popcornn consistently recovers more correct transition states than standard methods like nudged elastic band and growing string. Transition states govern chemical reaction rates and outcomes, and this method eliminates path discretization artifacts while reducing hyperparameter sensitivity, making high-throughput transition state search more robust and automation-ready. It could significantly impact computational chemistry and machine learning communities by providing a practical platform for MLIP-based path optimization. Popcornn leverages automatic differentiation from MLIPs to access second-order potential-energy-surface information, enabling non-local optimization to overcome local features. Its infrastructure is flexible in path objective, integration strategy, and optimization algorithm, though the study focuses on organic reactions and may require validation for other chemical systems.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Transition states are high-energy configurations that control chemical reaction pathways, but locating them is challenging. Double-ended search methods use reactant and product structures to find transition states, typically relying on discretized path representations developed for density functional theory (DFT), where energy evaluations are expensive. Machine-learned interatomic potentials (MLIPs) are data-driven models that predict energies and forces with near quantum accuracy at much lower cost, and they support automatic differentiation for efficient higher-order derivatives.

## [FailureGen: Synthetic Robot Failure Data via World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22367) ⭐️ 7.0/10

FailureGen introduces a four-stage pipeline that fine-tunes an action-conditioned Cosmos world model on real robot executions, then transfers failure-associated motion segments into successful host contexts to generate synthetic failure videos with temporal annotations. In a preliminary test, a failure detector trained on real plus generated data achieved an average precision of 0.51 versus 0.41 with real data alone, and detected 33 of 36 failure intervals compared with 28 of 36. Robot failures are scarce and expensive to collect at scale, which limits the coverage of failure scenarios in robot learning datasets and hampers downstream tasks like failure detection. By reusing observed failures in new contexts, FailureGen offers a scalable way to expand failure data and could improve the robustness of robot learning and safety monitoring. The method relies on a fine-tuned Cosmos world model and includes an annotation and filtering stage to ensure visual quality and event consistency; the evaluation is preliminary, covering only two implementations with different tasks and robot configurations, and visual failure detection is the sole downstream application tested.

**Background**: World models are learned simulators that predict future observations conditioned on actions, and NVIDIA's Cosmos is an open platform of world foundation models for physical AI. Action-conditioned world models can generate video rollouts for robotics, and prior work has shown that failure data scarcity limits vision-language model-based failure detection. ## [Round-Robin Dealing Optimizes Listwise LLM Reranking Schedules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22392) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proves that round-robin dealing of candidates by retriever rank minimizes expected cuts of true top passages in parallel listwise reranking schedules, and introduces DealRank, which achieves within 0.1 nDCG@10 of the best schedules while being 1.1–1.4x faster than BlitzRank. This work provides theoretical grounding and a practical scheduling algorithm that reduces expensive repair calls and latency in LLM-based reranking, directly benefiting information retrieval systems that rely on listwise rerankers for search and RAG pipelines. DealRank matches the best schedules within 0.1 nDCG@10 across six LLMs in two rounds, is consistently 1.1–1.4x faster than BlitzRank with up to a thousand candidates, and reduces repair calls from 59% to 14% of queries in existing schedules; random partitioning is equivalent at quota 4 but trails by 1.3 nDCG@10 at quota 1 over a dense retriever.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Listwise LLM rerankers use a large language model to reorder a set of candidate documents retrieved by a first-stage retriever, but they cannot handle hundreds of documents in one call. Parallel schedules split candidates into groups and advance a fixed quota from each group, and when a group contains more top passages than its quota, some are cut, requiring expensive repair calls. Round-robin dealing assigns candidates to groups in a rotating fashion based on retriever rank, similar to dealing cards, to balance the distribution of potentially relevant passages. nDCG@10 is a standard ranking quality metric that discounts gains by position and normalizes against the ideal ranking.

<a id="item-150"></a>
## [Gaze-RL Redistributes Rewards in Multimodal Reasoning via Cross-Modal Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22438) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Gaze-RL proposes a representation-driven policy optimization method that redistributes completion-level rewards in multimodal reasoning by inferring a latent reward landing field from the model's own cross-modal geometry and local predictive uncertainty. The method assigns larger update weight to positions that are both visually anchored and decision-bearing while preserving the mean-one property, and improves Qwen2.5-VL and InternVL3.5 across image and video benchmarks without process supervision or architectural changes. This work addresses a key challenge in multimodal reinforcement learning: outcome rewards are assigned to entire trajectories while visual evidence is sparse and uneven, leading to imprecise credit assignment. By eliminating the need for external annotators or process labels, Gaze-RL offers a more self-contained and potentially generalizable approach that could improve training efficiency and performance for vision-language models. The latent reward landing field is computed from internal cross-modal geometry and local predictive uncertainty, and it remains mean-one so that the total credit of each rollout is preserved while only its positionwise allocation changes. Gaze-RL requires no process supervision, auxiliary perception module, or architectural modification, making it easy to integrate into existing multimodal RL pipelines.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Multimodal post-training has evolved from broad instruction tuning to reinforcement learning for image and video reasoning, where outcome rewards can improve final-answer accuracy. However, these rewards are typically assigned to complete generated trajectories, while visual evidence is used only at sparse and uneven positions inside the response. Recent work has attempted to make multimodal RL more token-sensitive through grounding signals or external token-level weighting, but these approaches often rely on auxiliary annotators or process labels. Gaze-RL asks whether the allocation of the same trajectory-level reward can be inferred from the model's own multimodal computation, without external supervision.

## [Visual Backdoors Propagate Through On-Policy Distillation in VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22446) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.22446) reveals that visual backdoors can propagate from teacher to student models in On-Policy Distillation (OPD) for Vision-Language Models (VLMs), despite initial benign student behavior. The authors identify a critical poisoning threshold above which attack success rate rapidly approaches 100%, and propose a trigger design enabling backdoor propagation with zero explicit trigger injection. This challenges the intuition that OPD inherently resists backdoor propagation, highlighting a subtle security risk in a popular post-training paradigm for VLMs. The findings could affect anyone using OPD to train vision-language models, especially in safety-critical applications, and call for new defenses against such propagation. Unlike traditional offline distillation where backdoor propagation is gradual, OPD shows a threshold effect: backdoors rarely emerge at low poisoning ratios but rapidly reach near-100% success once a critical threshold is exceeded. Propagation is driven by the continuous accumulation of anomalous probability mass assigned by the teacher, not by explicit malicious responses, and the proposed trigger design achieves propagation without any explicit trigger injection.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: On-Policy Distillation (OPD) is a post-training method where a student model generates its own responses and the teacher provides dense token-level supervision via probability distributions, combining on-policy training with knowledge distillation. Vision-Language Models (VLMs) process both images and text, and visual backdoor attacks embed triggers into images to cause harmful behavior. Prior work showed backdoors can propagate through conventional knowledge distillation, but OPD's resistance was assumed due to its use of student-generated benign responses.

## [MAVE reconstructs editable 3D blockouts from film shots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22464) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MAVE introduces MAVEBench, a benchmark of 50 film shots and 416 keyframes, and a video-to-blockout framework that combines metric observation, agentic modeling, and evidence-based verification. On MAVEBench, MAVE improves overall scores by 27.5-41.1 points over vanilla baselines, achieving 73.2 with DeepSeek-1017 at USD 0.054 per shot while cutting reconstruction time by 53%. This work addresses the costly manual reconstruction of 3D blockouts used in previsualization and as structural conditions for video generation, potentially enabling creators to produce editable scenes more efficiently and generating paired training data at scale. The release of MAVEData-3K (3,000 blockout-video pairs) and code could accelerate research in video-to-3D and agentic 3D reconstruction. MAVE first recovers cameras, human motion, and geometric evidence in a shared metric coordinate system, then an agent constructs an editable environment under constraints, with programmatic checks reporting inconsistencies to guide refinement. Outputs are editable Blender scenes and frame-aligned blockout videos, and the method is evaluated across three agents, though it is an arXiv preprint without peer review or community discussion yet.

## [Planner-Hardware Co-Design Accelerates JEPA World Model Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22483) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose a planner-hardware co-design that exploits differential bit-level sparsity in quantized JEPA world models, using a novel reference forest sampling technique to manufacture sparsity. The co-design achieves 1.28-1.80x speedup and 38-52% energy reduction over a dense accelerator on three JEPA models. This work addresses the critical bottleneck of slow planning in embodied AI by tightly coupling algorithm and hardware, potentially enabling real-time control for robots using world models. It demonstrates a new paradigm where the planner actively shapes sparsity for hardware efficiency, which could influence future accelerator designs for model predictive control. The planner uses reference forest sampling to couple sampled action sequences to a shared root, increasing sparsity while maintaining planning quality. The bit-exact hardware exploits this sparsity, achieving 1.22-1.58x speedup and 16-36% energy reduction over prior bit-sparse designs with the same area.

Sampling-based model predictive control (MPC) uses repeated predictor inference to evaluate many action sequences, which is computationally expensive. Bit-level sparsity accelerators skip redundant bit operations in quantized neural networks, but typically rely on input data sparsity rather than planner-induced sparsity.

## [Motion-Aware Reward Model Fixes Motion Collapse in Video Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22495) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.22495) identifies 'motion collapse' in instruction-guided video editing reward models, where temporal consistency is favored over motion, and proposes a motion-aware reward model that combines an RGB frame-difference stream with preference training against motion-suppressed and fabricated-motion negatives. On the VE-Bench motion ladder, the model reduces the rate of selecting motion-suppressed candidates to 0% while achieving a Spearman correlation of 0.57 with human quality ratings. This work exposes a critical flaw in reward models used for video editing and generation, where optimizing for reward can inadvertently suppress motion, leading to static or unnatural outputs. The proposed fix could improve the quality and dynamism of AI-edited videos and influence how reward models are designed for generative video tasks. The composite reward prefers motion-suppressed candidates in 97% of cases on VE-Bench, and even learned video rewards and a VLM judge struggle with mild suppression. The proposed model uses a distillation anchor to preserve alignment with human quality judgments and detects unseen suppression variants while preserving motion during reward-guided fine-tuning on two editing pipelines.

## [Held-out action test reveals forecast accuracy can mislead world models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22537) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces a held-out action test for action-conditioned world models, where actions are excluded from training and scored against known outcomes, and applies it to retail pricing across 2,853 fitted models. It finds that forecast accuracy is a fair guide between different model kinds (rank correlation 0.32 to 0.66) but can reverse between versions of the same model (down to -0.69 in high-demand weeks), and that JEPA-style action-effect pretraining leaves error on new plans unchanged. This work challenges the common assumption that better forecast accuracy guarantees better generalization to unseen actions, which is critical for any domain where world models are used for planning under novel interventions. It provides a practical evaluation protocol that can be transferred to other action-conditioned settings such as healthcare dosing, and shows that simple price rules can improve robustness on extreme price cuts. In generated worlds, a log-linear model with price rules is best when its curve matches the truth, but the world model with rules outperforms it in 36 of 40 worlds where the curve lies outside the rules, even without its recurrent cell. On real data (Breakfast at the Frat, The Complete Journey, M5), the rules lower error on pure price cuts deeper than any seen (0.63 to 0.56 on the Frat; 0.45 to 0.44, within chance, on The Complete Journey) but not on lasting price moves, and a small share of random test plans helps every model slightly.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Action-conditioned world models predict how a system's state evolves in response to actions, and are trusted to answer "what if" questions about actions never seen in training. JEPA (Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture) is a self-supervised framework that learns by predicting abstract representations in latent space rather than reconstructing raw inputs, and here it is used as a pretraining method to learn action effects. Retail pricing serves as a testbed where the action is a price and promotion plan, and the goal is to evaluate whether models generalize to held-out plans.

## [JET fine-tuning curbs over-exploration in tool-calling code agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22539) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose Just-Enough Exploration Tuning (JET), a fine-tuning method that truncates training trajectories at the point where gathered information is sufficient and inserts a rationale explaining why the agent can answer, with higher loss weight on that rationale. Experiments on two repository-level QA benchmarks with two open-weight 8B models show JET reduces tool calls by 11–33% on self-generated trajectories while largely preserving scores, and by 18–28% versus SFT on expert trajectories while retaining most of SFT's score gain. Over-exploration wastes inference cost and latency in LLM agents that navigate code repositories, and standard supervised fine-tuning on expert trajectories propagates this inefficiency. JET offers a lightweight, targeted fix that improves agent efficiency without sacrificing much accuracy, which matters for deploying cost-effective repository-level QA agents. JET works by truncating each training trajectory at the step where information becomes sufficient, inserting a rationale that explains why answering is now possible, and upweighting the loss on that rationale during fine-tuning. The method is evaluated on two repository-level QA benchmarks using two open-weight 8B models, and it also lowers inference cost in both self-generated and expert-trajectory settings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Repository-level question answering requires an LLM agent to explore a code repository using tools such as file search and code reading, then answer once enough information is gathered. Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on expert trajectories is a common way to build such agents, but it can teach the model to mimic unnecessary tool calls present in the training data. Over-exploration refers to the agent continuing to invoke tools even after the gathered information is already sufficient to answer.

## [JointGV corrects visual world model drift with parallel graph dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22543) ⭐️ 7.0/10

JointGV is a plug-in method that rolls out a task-conditioned scene graph alongside a visual world model and couples their predicted changes to compute a bounded residual correction. Attached to DINO-WM and DreamerV3, it reduces joint-position error by 11–28% over 32-step rollouts and cuts real-robot pole-angle error from 12.56° to 6.69°. Recursive error accumulation is a key obstacle to using visual world models for long-horizon robot planning, and JointGV addresses it without retraining or restructuring the base model. Its plug-in design and demonstrated control improvement (46/50 episodes vs. 0/50) suggest a practical path to more reliable model-based robot control. The method relies on a cross-granularity dynamics hypothesis—that graph-node and visual-state changes describe the same physical transition—and keeps graph-learning and correction gradients from propagating into the original visual dynamics. Evaluations cover DMC Cheetah Run, Walker Walk, and Finger Turn Hard across three seeds, plus real-robot inverted-pendulum sequences.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Visual world models predict future visual states from actions, but small errors compound over long rollouts, degrading planning. Scene graphs represent objects and their relations symbolically, offering a complementary abstraction that may be more stable over time. JointGV couples these two representations to correct drift while leaving the visual model unchanged.

## [Narrative Coherence Boosts LLM Honeypot Deception Against Autonomous Attackers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22589) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.22589) proposes 'narrative deception,' a reactive honeypot strategy that maps each attacker command to a MITRE ATT&CK technique and deploys mutually referential artefacts (files, processes, services, logs) on an emulated Ubuntu host during an ongoing intrusion. In 1,150 SSH sessions with LLM attackers and defenders, coherent scenarios raised the median session length of advanced attackers from 40 to 165 commands and reduced abandonment from 86% to 52%. As autonomous LLM-driven offensive agents become realistic adversaries, this work shows that deception quality—not just emulator fidelity—is key to retaining them, which could reshape how honeypots are designed and evaluated. The findings suggest defenders should focus on sustaining meaningful findings to keep attackers engaged rather than merely mimicking real systems. The study compares four conditions (Static, Same-TTP, Multi-TTP, Chain) across four open-weight attacker models, four defender models, and three attacker skill levels, finding that engagement increases significantly at every step with comparable inference cost. Nearly all sessions ended due to loss of interest rather than detection, highlighting that sustained meaningful findings are crucial for deception.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Honeypots are decoy systems designed to attract and analyze attackers. MITRE ATT&CK is a knowledge base of adversary tactics and techniques, while MITRE Engage is a framework for planning adversary engagement operations. LLMs now enable dynamic honeypot responses, but prior work evaluates generated content one reply at a time, leaving open whether coherent multi-artefact narratives better retain attackers.

NS-Gym is introduced as the first simulation toolkit explicitly designed for non-stationary Markov decision processes (NS-MDPs), integrated within the Gymnasium framework. It separates the evolution of environmental parameters from the agent's decision-making module, enabling modular and flexible modeling of dynamic environments, and includes standardized interfaces and benchmark problems. This fills a gap in reinforcement learning research by providing the first standardized framework for reproducible evaluation of algorithms under non-stationary conditions, which are common in real-world applications. It could accelerate research and benchmarking in adaptive decision-making, benefiting researchers and practitioners working on dynamic environments. Non-stationary MDPs extend this by allowing environmental dynamics to change over time, which is more realistic but harder to solve. Gymnasium is a popular open-source Python library that provides a standard API for reinforcement learning environments, widely used for developing and comparing RL algorithms.
