## [AI reasoning effort mirrors human difficulty on content moderation tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20262) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv study (2508.20262v3) compared six large reasoning models with 1,854 human subjects on a content moderation task and found that models expend more chain-of-thought effort on cases that humans find harder, even though the task is subjective and lacks verifiable answers. Specifically, when offensiveness was held constant across two posts, humans took 31% longer and models produced 51% to 306% longer chains of thought. This suggests that reasoning traces from large reasoning models could be used to flag and prioritize difficult content moderation cases for human review, improving interpretability and efficiency in moderation systems. It also provides evidence that human-like effort allocation emerges in AI even on subjective tasks, which is relevant for aligning AI with human judgment in high-stakes domains. The correlation between individual human response times and model reasoning effort is relatively low (r = 0.15–0.19), but when tasks are grouped by types of offensive language, average response times and average reasoning effort are highly correlated (r = 0.85–0.93). Qualitative analysis of Gemini 2.5 Pro's chain-of-thought summaries shows that secondary factors were mentioned more often when offensiveness was fixed.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Large reasoning models (LRMs) like OpenAI's o1 and Gemini 2.5 generate intermediate reasoning steps, known as chain-of-thought (CoT), before answering, which helps on complex problems. Content moderation is the process of identifying and removing harmful or offensive user-generated content, and it often involves subjective judgments. Previous research has shown that LRMs spend more effort on problems that humans take longer to solve, but it was unclear if this holds for subjective tasks without clear answers.

<a id="item-162"></a>
## [AutoPBO: LLM-powered Optimization for Local Search PBO Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04007) ⭐️ 7.0/10

AutoPBO is a novel LLM-powered framework that automatically enhances the internal heuristics of local search solvers for pseudo-Boolean optimization (PBO). It was evaluated on four public benchmarks, including a real-world benchmark, a PB competition benchmark, an integer linear programming benchmark, and a crafted combinatorial benchmark, and compared against six state-of-the-art solvers: NuPBO, OraSLS, PBO-IHS, RoundingSat, Gurobi, and SCIP. This work demonstrates that LLMs can automate the design of heuristics for PBO local search solvers, significantly improving performance over previous local search approaches while remaining competitive with state-of-the-art complete and MIP solvers. It suggests a promising direction for reducing expert effort in solver development and could impact the broader field of combinatorial optimization. AutoPBO is evaluated on four diverse benchmarks and compared with six state-of-the-art competitors, including two local search PBO solvers (NuPBO and OraSLS), two complete PB solvers (PBO-IHS and RoundingSat), and two MIP solvers (Gurobi and SCIP). The results show significant improvements over previous local search approaches while maintaining competitive performance against the other solvers.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Pseudo-Boolean Optimization (PBO) is a framework for modeling combinatorial problems using pseudo-Boolean constraints, where variables are Boolean and constraints are linear inequalities over these variables. Local search solvers are effective for PBO but rely heavily on hand-crafted heuristics that require expert effort to design and tune. Large Language Models (LLMs) have recently shown potential in automating algorithm design, but their application to PBO solver optimization has not been explored until AutoPBO.

<a id="item-163"></a>
## [CORE: Concept-Oriented Reinforcement Learning Bridges Definition-Application Gap in Math Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.18857) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced CORE (Concept-Oriented REinforcement), a reinforcement learning framework that uses explicit concept supervision to improve large language models' conceptual application in mathematical reasoning. Starting from a low-contamination textbook resource linking exercises to concept descriptions, CORE synthesizes concept-aligned quizzes, injects concept snippets during rollouts, and reinforces conceptual reasoning via trajectory replacement or a forward-KL constraint, yielding consistent gains over vanilla and SFT baselines on in-domain and out-of-domain math benchmarks. This work addresses a known weakness of RLVR pipelines—they reinforce final answers but provide little fine-grained conceptual signal, so models learn pattern reuse rather than genuine understanding. CORE's algorithm- and verifier-agnostic approach offers a practical way to add conceptual supervision to existing RL training, potentially improving LLMs' mathematical reasoning and transfer to novel problems. CORE first quantifies the definition-application gap via a sanity probe showing LLMs can restate definitions but fail concept-linked quizzes. It then unifies direct training on concept-aligned quizzes and concept-injected rollouts under outcome regularization, using either trajectory replacement after group failures, a lightweight forward-KL constraint aligning unguided with concept-primed policies, or standard GRPO on concept-aligned quizzes.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) is a popular paradigm for training LLMs on reasoning tasks, where rewards come from automatically verifiable outcomes such as correct final answers. While effective, RLVR provides only outcome-level supervision, so models may learn to reuse patterns without grasping underlying concepts. The definition-application gap refers to the phenomenon where models can recite definitions but fail to apply them when solving problems that require genuine understanding.

## [GCD: GCM-consistent diffusion enables zero-shot climate downscaling across heterogeneous GCMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.21760) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces GCD (GCM-consistent Diffusion for Zero-shot Downscaling), a diffusion-based method that combines geographic conditioning, spectrally filtered measurement guidance, and distillation-accelerated Conjugate Gradient guidance to perform zero-shot climate downscaling. It reduces inference from 1,000 diffusion steps to 6 while maintaining consistency with coarse GCM inputs, and achieves competitive 99th-percentile extreme-event accuracy across five heterogeneous GCMs without model-specific retraining. This work addresses a key challenge in climate downscaling: generalizing across heterogeneous GCMs without paired high-resolution training data. By enabling zero-shot downscaling with a 167x reduction in inference steps, GCD makes high-resolution climate projections more practical and accessible for impact studies across multiple climate models. GCD uses a high-resolution climate prior with inference-time constraints from coarse GCM inputs, and its distillation-accelerated Conjugate Gradient guidance maintains consistency while drastically cutting sampling steps. The method reconstructs plausible high-frequency tropical cyclone structures and is evaluated on five heterogeneous GCMs, with code available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: General Circulation Models (GCMs) simulate the climate system at coarse spatial resolutions, so downscaling is needed to produce finer-scale data for local impact studies. Traditional supervised downscaling requires paired high-resolution data, which is often unavailable or synthetic, limiting generalization across different GCMs. Diffusion models are generative models that learn to sample from complex distributions and have been adapted for inverse problems via posterior sampling, but standard diffusion posterior sampling is computationally expensive and can struggle with consistency.

## [EmbeWebAgent embeds web agents directly into enterprise UIs via frontend hooks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.14865) ⭐️ 7.0/10

EmbeWebAgent is a new framework that embeds web agents directly into existing enterprise UIs using lightweight frontend hooks (curated ARIA and URL-based observations, plus a per-page function registry exposed via WebSocket) and a reusable backend workflow for reasoning and action. It is stack-agnostic (e.g., React or Angular), supports mixed-granularity actions from GUI primitives to higher-level composites, and orchestrates navigation, manipulation, and domain-specific analytics via MCP tools. Most web agents rely on brittle screenshot or DOM-level control, which limits robustness and expressiveness; by leveraging application-level access available in enterprise settings, EmbeWebAgent enables more reliable multi-step automation and could accelerate practical agent deployment in enterprise software. The demo shows minimal retrofitting effort and multi-step behaviors grounded in a live UI setting, but it is a demo paper with limited evaluation and no community discussion provided. The approach requires explicit control of both frontend and backend, which is feasible in enterprise settings but not on arbitrary websites.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Web agents typically interact with applications at the human interface level, observing screenshots or raw DOM trees without application-level access. ARIA (Accessible Rich Internet Applications) is a W3C specification that defines roles and attributes to enhance web accessibility, and its attributes can provide structured observations for agents. MCP (Model Context Protocol), introduced by Anthropic in November 2024, is an open standard for connecting AI systems to external tools and data sources, enabling orchestration of domain-specific tools.

## [PRCO: Dual-role RL framework coevolves perception and reasoning in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.28618) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PRCO introduces a dual-role reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) framework with a shared policy, where an Observer generates question-tailored evidence captions and a Solver predicts the final answer from those captions. The Observer is optimized with a utility reward derived from the Solver's downstream success, while the Solver uses standard outcome rewards, yielding over 7 points average accuracy gain across eight multimodal reasoning benchmarks compared to the base model. This work addresses the perception bottleneck in multimodal LLM reasoning, where shared outcome rewards blur credit assignment and often improve reasoning patterns without reliably enhancing visual evidence extraction. By explicitly separating and coevolving perception and reasoning, PRCO offers a training paradigm that could improve grounding and accuracy for multimodal models across scales. PRCO uses a shared policy with two cooperative roles and role-specific rewards: the Solver receives verifiable outcome rewards on the final answer, while the Observer receives a utility reward based on the Solver's downstream success. Experiments across eight challenging benchmarks show consistent gains over 7 points on average across model scales, outperforming prior open-source RL-tuned baselines.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is a post-training method that fine-tunes models using automatic, rule-based checkers as rewards, and it has substantially improved reasoning in large language models. In multimodal LLMs, however, standard RLVR typically applies a single outcome-based reward to the entire rollout, which creates a credit assignment problem: the model cannot tell whether success came from correctly extracting visual evidence or from reasoning over it. The credit assignment problem refers to the challenge of attributing a final reward to the specific intermediate actions or steps that led to it.

## [CauSim Turns Scarce Causal Labels into Scalable LLM Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09079) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced CauSim, a framework that has LLMs incrementally build executable structural causal models (SCMs) of increasing complexity, converting causal reasoning from a scarce-label problem into a scalable supervised one. The framework formalizes non-executable causal knowledge into code for data augmentation and translates executable SCMs into natural language to supervise previously hard-to-supervise representations. Causal reasoning remains a known weakness of LLMs despite strong performance on math and coding, and CauSim offers a way to generate verifiable, scalable supervision data without costly human labeling. This could improve LLM reliability in domains requiring causal understanding and enable self-improvement through self-generated simulators. CauSim demonstrates generalization across representations, consistent gains from curriculum scaling and increased data volume, LLM self-improvement via self-generated simulators, and data augmentation by formalizing existing domain knowledge. The framework maintains verifiable answers to causal queries even as simulators scale to globally complex systems.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Structural causal models (SCMs) are mathematical formalisms that represent variables through structural equations informed by a directed acyclic graph (DAG), encoding hypothesized causal relationships. They allow researchers to answer causal questions from observational data and are widely used in epidemiology, machine learning, and signal processing. However, causal systems are often complex and expressed in non-executable forms, and ground-truth answers to causal queries are scarce, making it hard to train LLMs on causal reasoning.

## [CardioLoom: Report-Guided ECG Foundation Model for Cross-Domain Phenotyping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25446) ⭐️ 7.0/10

CardioLoom is a report-guided ECG foundation model pretrained on 2,781,289 ECG-report pairs from 1,298,359 patients and evaluated across 89 targets, including nine external cohorts. Using labels from only 10% of training patients, it achieved a macro-AUPRC of 0.559, surpassing fully supervised training with all labels (0.549). This work demonstrates that large-scale ECG-report pretraining enables label-efficient cardiovascular diagnosis and shows strong external generalization, which could reduce annotation burden and improve deployment of ECG AI across diverse clinical settings. It also highlights variable transfer to echocardiographic phenotypes, informing where such models are most reliable. Across 45 internal ECG diagnoses, CardioLoom achieved a macro-AUPRC of 0.624 versus 0.615 for DeepECG-SSL, and across 203 external evaluations, means were 0.425 and 0.424. For heart failure with reduced ejection fraction, AUPRCs were 0.690, 0.641, and 0.677 in Zhongshan, Shiyuan, and BIDMC, while cardiac amyloidosis screening yielded only 4.5% positive predictive value at a threshold targeting 99% specificity.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Foundation models are large neural networks pretrained on broad data using self-supervised or transfer learning, then adapted to many downstream tasks. In ECG analysis, routine clinical reports can serve as scalable weak supervision, allowing models to learn cardiovascular representations without costly manual labels. AUPRC (area under the precision-recall curve) is a metric suited to imbalanced classification, where higher values indicate better performance.

## [OPT*: A Scalable Benchmark for Optimization-Style Reasoning in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05464) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces OPT*, a scalable family of optimization-style tasks for training and evaluating LLM step-by-step reasoning over expanding search spaces. It proposes two training regimes: solver-guided online policy optimization with rank-based reward shaping, and search-based offline RL when solvers are unavailable. This work extends verifiable-reward training beyond math and coding to real-world planning tasks that require finding high-value feasible solutions among many alternatives. It provides a scalable benchmark with a complexity axis, enabling systematic study of LLM optimization-like reasoning and potentially improving decision-making in complex domains. Each OPT* task includes a feasibility checker and evaluator, and a complexity parameter expands the search space without new human labels. Theoretically, the paper relates success in large search spaces to the information a reasoner extracts per unit of search budget, and empirically ablates ingredients that make search efficient.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Verifiable reward training, such as reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR), has improved LLM reasoning in domains like math and coding by using automatically checkable outcomes as rewards. However, many real-world tasks require step-by-step decision making to find a high-value feasible plan among many valid alternatives, which these domains do not fully capture. OPT* addresses this gap by providing a scalable benchmark family for optimization-style reasoning, where a complexity parameter controls the search space size. The paper also explores two RL approaches: solver-guided online policy optimization and search-based offline RL.

## [LAGO: Latent Goal Prediction from Language for Model-Based Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20627) ⭐️ 7.0/10

LAGO introduces a hierarchical world model that uses a single predictor to both forecast action-conditioned dynamics and ground language instructions as sequences of intermediate latent subgoals, trained with a single regression objective in a shared latent space. At each planning step, it predicts latent subgoals from language and optimizes actions via a soft-minimum alignment cost, repredicting subgoals as the state evolves. This addresses a key bottleneck in JEPA-based agents by unifying dynamics forecasting and language grounding in one model, avoiding noisy cross-modal alignment or dependence on separate large generative models. It enables more effective long-horizon planning from language instructions, with potential impact on robotics and reinforcement learning. LAGO outperforms flat and hierarchical image-goal planners across navigation and manipulation environments, with the largest gains on long-horizon navigation with curved, hazard-constrained paths, more than doubling the success rate of a goal-conditioned behavior cloning policy. Grounding language in the world model's own latent space also outperforms vision-language reward models, including one that plans with ground-truth dynamics.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) enable agents to plan in latent space by predicting the outcomes of candidate actions, but specifying tasks remains a challenge. Visual targets provide precise local gradients but poor distant guidance, while language is flexible but limited by noisy cross-modal alignment or dependence on separate large generative models. Model-based reinforcement learning uses a learned model of the environment to plan actions, and language grounding connects natural language instructions to actionable representations.

## [PASS: Shaping Process Advantages for GRPO in LLM Reasoners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29296) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces PASS (Process Advantage Signal Shaping), a method that converts dense process supervision scores into policy advantages for GRPO by separately normalizing and fusing reward channels, constructing batch-shared credit units, and normalizing return-to-go by remaining unit count. Across seven math benchmarks, PASS improves average pass@1 by 4.62 percentage points over outcome-only GRPO with roughly 2% shorter responses. This work shows that how local process scores are converted into advantages is a consequential design choice, not a mere implementation detail, and it offers a concrete recipe that improves reasoning performance across both learned PRM feedback and teacher-derived token feedback. It matters for researchers and practitioners training LLM reasoners with process-supervised reinforcement learning. PASS is evaluated with learned process reward model (PRM) feedback for mathematical reasoning and teacher-derived token feedback for multi-hop QA, and matched comparisons with Step-GRPO plus component ablations support the shared credit units and residual normalization. On multi-hop QA, token-level accumulation slightly favors pass@1 while PASS achieves higher pass@8/16 with shorter responses, revealing an accuracy–length trade-off.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) is a critic-free reinforcement learning algorithm for LLMs that samples a group of completions per prompt and computes advantages relative to the group's rewards, avoiding a separate value model. Process Reward Models (PRM) assign fine-grained scores to intermediate reasoning steps rather than only the final outcome, but it is non-trivial to turn these local scores into per-token policy advantages. Advantage shaping refers to modifying or constructing advantage signals to guide learning more effectively.

<a id="item-172"></a>
## [TTCov: Test-Conditioned Data Curation Aligns Training with Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22697) ⭐️ 7.0/10

TTCov introduces a data-level test-conditioned curation method that uses test-side information before training to select training data matching the deployment distribution, rather than updating model weights at inference. It builds a task Atlas of LLM-based atomic propositions and a Knowledge Atlas (K-Atlas) to represent coverage and distribution, then selects a budgeted training set whose deployment AP distribution approximates the target. This approach keeps adaptation off the inference path, avoiding the computational overhead and potential instability of test-time adaptation while still aligning training data with deployment conditions. It could benefit deployed AI systems, especially in autonomous driving, where test distributions are known in advance and data curation is critical. TTCov decomposes deployment-conditioned curation into coverage and distribution: coverage is represented by a task Atlas of atomic propositions seeded from open task knowledge and expanded with unmatched APs from unlabeled deployment samples, while distribution is operationalized as a K-Atlas with matched AP frequencies. Experiments on autonomous driving show better deployment-relevant coverage, closer K-Atlas matching, and stronger end-to-end driving performance than baselines, including city-to-city expansion.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Test-time adaptation (TTA) is a machine learning paradigm where a deployed model adapts to distribution shifts at inference using unlabeled test samples, but it requires updating model weights during inference. Data curation for deployment aims to select training data that matches the test distribution, but standard methods score training-side criteria rather than directly optimizing deployment match. TTCov proposes using test-side information before training to curate data, avoiding inference-time adaptation.

## [EMRD Benchmark Exposes VLM Weaknesses in Safety-Critical Embodied Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08077) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced EMRD, a benchmark that decomposes vision-language model spatial reasoning into Explore, Map, Remember, and Decide stages with 18 metrics, and evaluated 11 open- and closed-weight VLMs across 16 buildings under nominal and stressed conditions (darkness, smoke, motion noise). Open-weight models left rooms unvisited in 95% of episodes and detected at most 51% of objects, while closed-weight models explored more but often stopped prematurely even with an external notepad. This work provides actionable evidence that current VLMs are not yet reliable as embodied controllers in safety-critical scenarios, which matters for robotics deployment in search-and-rescue, inspection, and emergency response. The finding that open-weight models perform near chance on room selection and are biased by semantic labels highlights fundamental limitations in spatial memory and grounding that must be addressed before real-world use. The benchmark reveals that motion noise increases object position error by 12-60% for closed-weight models, strong darkness lowers map recall by 28-31 percentage points, and dense smoke reduces seen objects by 25-39 points, while the final room choice among entered rooms does not change significantly. Additionally, swapping a room's label is often enough to change the final decision, showing strong semantic prior bias.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) combine visual perception with language understanding and are increasingly used as the 'brain' of embodied agents, such as robots that navigate unknown spaces and make decisions. Open-weight models have publicly available parameters and can be freely modified, while closed-weight models are proprietary and accessed only via APIs. Spatial reasoning benchmarks like EMRD test whether these models can build and use internal maps over long horizons, a capability distinct from answering questions about static images.

## [Larger LLM Agents Resist Stale Notes but Fall for Newer-Looking Ones](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01852) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.01852v2) evaluates stale-evidence reliance across Qwen3 0.6B, 1.7B, 4B, and 8B checkpoints using action-scored Benefit and Safety suites, finding that larger checkpoints resist a plain stale note but incur substantial harm when the stale information appears newer. A full 2×2×2×2 factorial manipulating label, recency, source authority, and position reveals stronger recency effects in larger checkpoints and a position-effect sign reversal within Qwen3. This work shows that scaling model size does not uniformly improve robustness to stale memory in persistent-memory agents, and that recency cues can make larger models more vulnerable, which matters for anyone deploying memory-equipped agents in safety-critical settings. It also suggests that simple defenses like metadata and pre-resolution can substantially close the gap. Benefit-suite reliance on stored information is 0.92–1.00 across all checkpoints, while in the Safety suite metadata improves accuracy at all four sizes with larger gains at 4B/8B, and pre-resolution yields near-ceiling accuracy. Llama-Instruct comparisons provide cross-family evidence for selected effects, and RGB and MisBench corroborate stale-value reliance, though the study is limited to tested Qwen3 scales and does not isolate capability or evaluate a complete memory lifecycle.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Persistent-memory agents store information across interactions so they can recall facts later, but this continuity also lets outdated or poisoned content persist and influence decisions. Prior work such as the STALE benchmark has shown that LLM assistants can recognize a memory is out of date yet still act on it, and memory-poisoning studies show that malicious content can evade defenses. Qwen3 is Alibaba Cloud's family of open-weight language models spanning small to large sizes, making it a common testbed for scaling studies.

## [Code4Scene benchmarks coding agents on 3D scene construction and editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36777) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Code4Scene introduces a benchmark of 170 Unreal Engine cases built from human-assembled scenes to evaluate coding agents on two tasks: constructing 3D scenes from open-ended language specifications and precisely editing scene state from reference images. Across 14 coding-agent systems on the 83-case public set, construction and editing performance are strongly correlated (Spearman ρ = 0.67) but not interchangeable, with Claude Fable 5.1 leading construction and Gemini 3.8 Flash leading editing. This benchmark addresses a gap in evaluating spatial reasoning and state control of coding agents, showing that plausible 3D generation can hide incorrect spatial relations or unintended modifications. It matters for AI/ML and agent research because it scores engine-native scene state rather than code or renders, providing a more reliable measure of whether agents truly understand 3D structure. The benchmark evaluates generated scenes for task fulfillment, artifact integrity, and static physical validity, with edits compared against withheld ground truth. Results show Spatial Composition is the weakest construction category for every agent, and editing remains imprecise: the best Repair F1 is only 0.562, with 34.2% of edits that fully recover the target still introducing unintended changes elsewhere.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

## [Mingbird Harness Boosts Small Open Models' Multi-Step Task Completion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02001) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Mingbird is an open-source Python harness (12k lines, 628 tests) that adds procedural control and executable completion checks, enabling 1-9B parameter open models on consumer GPUs to complete real multi-step tasks. Its evaluation shows a 0.886 artifact score versus 0.631, 0.479, and 0.405 for three open scaffolds on a 288-cell matrix, and it ranks first or tied first in all three domains on τ2-bench. This addresses a critical gap for local-first agents: small models often stop prematurely, repeat actions, or falsely claim success, and the harness supplies the procedural control that large models provide internally. It could make capable AI agents practical on consumer hardware without cloud dependence. The design centers on completion control: the harness runs executable checks on the working directory and refuses completion claims when they fail. Disabling five of six completion-time checks costs 0.152 of artifact score on 18 held-out tasks (95% CI [+0.064, +0.246], Holm p = 0.014), and a configuration-surface audit found four defects missed by the 625-test suite of v3.1.1.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: An agent harness is the software layer around an LLM that handles tool execution, memory, context management, and state persistence, effectively turning a text generator into an agent. Small language models (1-9B parameters) can run locally on laptops but lack the self-control of larger models, making harness design crucial for reliable multi-step work.

## [Paper Demystifies Data Generation and Verification Challenges in Terminal-Agent Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02405) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.02405v2) presents a pipeline that generates containerized terminal tasks and executable verifiers, and uses it to identify four distinct requirements—structural validity, verifier faithfulness, policy solvability, and infrastructure reliability—that are often conflated in automated terminal-agent training. In a GRPO training of Qwen3.5-9B, the model overfits on an easy dataset within 20 steps, while adding hard tasks lowers mean rollout pass@2 from 81.3% to 20.6%, restoring informative failures without changing the training configuration. The findings highlight that a runnable Docker image and executable test suite do not guarantee faithful or informative rewards, which is critical for researchers using reinforcement learning to train terminal agents. The paper calls for a terminal-agent-specific evaluation harness that pairs structural checks and rollout-derived solvability with extensive verifier audits, potentially shaping best practices in agent training. The paper identifies near-duplicate task generation, incomplete anti-shortcut safeguards, Dockerfile failures, asynchronous pass@k bias, and concurrency-induced environment errors as distinct threats to measurement. These issues can distort training signals even when tasks appear structurally valid.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Terminal agents are AI systems that interact with command-line environments to perform tasks like coding and system administration. Training them with reinforcement learning (RL) requires automatically generated tasks and verifiers that convert open-ended interactions into reward signals. GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) is an RL algorithm that samples a group of answers per prompt and uses the group's average reward as a baseline, making training more efficient. However, the reliability of generated tasks and verifiers is often assumed rather than validated, leading to potential failure modes.

## [CISE uses conformal intervals to make LLM self-evolving search reliable under imperfect proxy rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02975) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose Conformal Interval-Driven Self-Evolution (CISE), which constructs candidate-specific reward intervals using conditional conformal inference and iteration-wise online density-ratio estimation. CISE uses conservative interval-based rewards for evolutionary feedback and only returns candidates when all required property intervals lie entirely within feasible regions. This addresses a key challenge in LLM-based scientific discovery: when high-fidelity evaluation is expensive, imperfect proxy rewards can assign high scores to infeasible candidates, contaminating both final outputs and evolutionary feedback. CISE's statistically calibrated intervals provide a way to reduce false positives and improve reliability, which is crucial for downstream validation with limited budgets. CISE derives fixed-iteration coverage results under explicit assumptions of independence and covariate shift. In experiments on three materials science self-evolving search tasks, all candidates returned by CISE are true positives under high-fidelity evaluation, whereas baselines return more candidates but include false positives.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: LLM-based self-evolving search is a promising approach for scientific discovery, where a model iteratively generates and refines candidates. Conformal inference is a statistical framework that produces prediction intervals with rigorous coverage guarantees for any black-box predictor. Density-ratio estimation directly estimates the ratio of two probability densities from data, useful for covariate shift adjustment. Proxy rewards are low-cost approximations of true evaluation, often used when high-fidelity evaluation is prohibitively expensive.

## [Realtime-Bench: Frontier Models Struggle with Real-Time Interactive Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22155) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new benchmark called Realtime-Bench evaluates agents on dynamic tasks where delayed actions directly affect success, and finds that under Wall Clock conditions (where environments advance according to actual inference latency), the best model achieves only 44% success rate and 36% Pass^3. The benchmark also introduces Paused Clock and Token Clock to separate serving latency from models' real-time streaming capability. This benchmark addresses a gap in evaluating real-time streaming models by focusing on effective action under strict temporal constraints, rather than just conversational quality or streaming perception. The low success rates highlight significant challenges and could drive research toward more temporally aware and adaptive AI systems. The benchmark compares Wall Clock (realistic latency) with Paused Clock (idealized, no inference delays) and Token Clock (simulated time based on generated tokens) to isolate temporal awareness from physical serving speed. Results show that higher reasoning effort has mixed effects under time pressure, reflecting a trade-off between decision quality and timely execution.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Real-time interaction requires agents to perceive evolving environments and act under temporal constraints, but existing evaluations of real-time streaming models focus on conversational quality and streaming perception, leaving effective action under strict temporal constraints untested. Recently, models like GPT-Realtime and Gemini Live have made real-time streaming practically possible, but their ability to act effectively under time pressure remains unclear. Realtime-Bench is a suite of carefully curated dynamic tasks where delayed actions directly affect success, designed to test this capability.

## [Agentic In-Context Learning: What Algorithms Can Transformers Learn in Closed-Loop Interaction?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23051) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Agentic In-Context Learning (Agentic ICL), a regime where a fixed-parameter Transformer's actions determine what evidence enters its context next. It constructs causal Transformers that approximate interactive learning algorithms (active learning, bandits, online RL) over a finite horizon with bounded return loss, and characterizes when optimizing process rewards preserves final-outcome-optimal behavior. This provides a theoretical learning-algorithm view for tool-using language model agents, bridging ICL theory with interactive decision-making and offering expressivity and training guarantees that could guide RL post-training of agents. It matters for AI/ML researchers designing agentic systems and reward schemes. The framework represents an interactive learner by its state, update, and decision rule; distillation experiments show Transformers can learn to execute three representative recursions, and models trained from outcome versus process rewards without oracle action or state targets yield distinct decision policies whose behavioral differences persist in a pretrained language model across textual paraphrases.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: In-context learning (ICL) refers to a fixed-parameter Transformer learning from examples supplied in its prompt, and prior work has analyzed it as implicit gradient descent or as an algorithm. Language model agents extend this by interacting with an environment, so their actions shape future context, a closed-loop setting the paper calls Agentic ICL. Process reward models score intermediate steps while outcome rewards score only final answers, and this distinction is central to the paper's training analysis.

## [Transformers Forget Their Prompt as a Power Law, No-Hair Theorem Shows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24133) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.24133v2) proves a conditional no-hair theorem for transformers, showing that the difference in hidden states caused by two different prompts decays approximately as a power law t^{-α} with the number of generated tokens t. The authors derive a bound of O(P(log t)^{L-1}/t) for finite-depth pre-LN softmax transformers and find that trained models forget faster (α≈0.55–0.7) than untrained ones (α≈0.2–0.5). This work offers a theoretical explanation for why large language models can lose track of their initial prompt as generation proceeds, linking it to the black hole no-hair theorem and providing a measurable exponent α. It could influence how researchers design context handling, prompt engineering, and long-context architectures, and the released toolkit allows measuring α for any Hugging Face causal LM. The bound is asymptotic and uninformative at the lengths measured in trained networks; an exactly solvable linear relay model shows the bound cannot be improved and accounts for untrained curves. Cancelling dilution with a logit bias on the prompt stops decay early in training, and training suppresses relay by about one billion tokens before attention sinks form.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: The black hole no-hair theorem states that a black hole is completely characterized by a few conserved charges (mass, charge, spin), forgetting all other details of the matter that formed it. Transformers are the dominant architecture for language models, using attention mechanisms and layer normalization (pre-LN places normalization before sublayers for stable training). This paper asks whether a transformer similarly retains only limited information about its prompt after generating many tokens, and proves a power-law decay under certain conditions.

## [ABACUS: Program-Guided Multistep Reasoning over Frozen Judgment Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25963) ⭐️ 7.0/10

ABACUS introduces a framework where a generative teacher designs composition programs offline and frozen judgment models resolve local semantic questions online, enabling multistep reasoning. On three BIG-Bench Hard tasks, nine independently constructed programs solved all 450 executions of 150 development inputs, and three refined programs achieved 99.67% macro accuracy on 600 held-out questions, compared to 64.00% for direct Kev and 68.80% for final-answer-only Qwen3.5-4B. This work shows that reusable programs can substantially expand the reasoning capability of a compact judgment model while keeping its parameters fixed, offering a path to efficient multistep reasoning without retraining large models. It could impact AI/ML research on program synthesis and reasoning, especially for resource-constrained deployments. The framework was instantiated with Kev, and the programs achieved comparable observed accuracy to Qwen's unrestricted response policy (99.07%) with an 8.43-fold online speedup: 0.255 versus 2.150 seconds per question. Evaluation is limited to three BIG-Bench Hard tasks and the abstract is truncated, so broader generalization remains to be tested.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Judgment models are systems that answer semantic questions by scoring a finite set of candidate outcomes, often used as evaluators in LLM-as-a-judge setups. BIG-Bench Hard (BBH) is a curated subset of 23 challenging BIG-Bench tasks designed to test multi-step reasoning, where models must chain several inference steps. Program synthesis is the task of automatically constructing programs from specifications, and compositional program synthesis assembles programs from reusable modules. ABACUS combines these ideas by having a generative teacher design composition programs that orchestrate frozen judgment models.

A new arXiv paper (2610.26836) shows that when LLM agents access long PHREEQC geochemistry simulator reports via a section index instead of having them in context, GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2 lose 11.3 and 7.9 accuracy points on 158 execution-verified multiple-choice items, while Claude Opus 4.6 and Claude Sonnet 4.6 show no detectable cost. ## [RIPPLE trains coding agents to recover from failures via process-level policy learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31276) ⭐️ 7.0/10

RIPPLE is a recovery-oriented training framework that internalizes process-level recovery guidance into a single self-recovering coding agent policy, eliminating the need for a deployment-time critic. Across four repository-level software-engineering benchmarks, it improves the base policy by an arithmetic mean of 4.8 score points. This work addresses the Critique-to-Action Handoff Gap, where long-horizon coding agents can diagnose a failure yet fail to change course, a key obstacle to reliable autonomous software engineering. The gains come without any auxiliary critic at deployment, making the approach more practical for real-world agentic coding systems. A prefix-only teacher provides evidence-grounded recovery supervision without access to future actions or terminal outcomes, followed by lightweight distillation and reinforcement learning that assigns stage-specific credit to reflection triggering, diagnosis, and corrective action. Local validity signals gate trajectory advantages so intermediate recovery decisions do not indiscriminately inherit terminal outcomes, and gains concentrate where the prefix contains sufficient diagnostic evidence and the repository state remains reversible within the remaining budget.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Long-horizon coding agents operate over many steps in real repositories, and prior work has shown they can generate plausible critiques of a failure yet continue along the same incorrect trajectory. Process-level supervision and reinforcement learning with credit assignment are active research directions for improving such agents, while distillation from a teacher model is a common way to inject guidance into a student policy. RIPPLE combines these ideas to internalize recovery behavior directly into the agent's policy.

## [MatOS: An Agent Operating System for Materials Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34203) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers present MatOS, an agent operating system for materials research that organizes data, knowledge, tools, skills, roles, and workflows as reusable assets in a layered scientific asset graph, runs tasks as stateful adaptive execution graphs with scientific checks, and evolves assets from execution records and user feedback. On ResearchClawBench it improves over the previous best agent by 11.15% with the same base model, and it outperforms Codex CLI and Claude Code on MatTools and SciAgentGym. This work shows that treating research capabilities as reusable, evolving assets lets LLM agents carry out long, multistage materials studies spanning literature analysis, computation, and experiment, which could make AI-for-science agents more extensible, traceable, and reliable. It also extends the cost-score Pareto frontier, suggesting practical gains for autonomous laboratory and simulation workflows. In eight research cases MatOS narrowed more than 30 million candidate structures to 97 solid-electrolyte candidates, identified 89 halide variants with higher computed conductivity than their parent structures, linked force errors of 11 pretrained interatomic potentials to local atomic environments, and supported two new methods reducing errors by 3.1% and 13.6% versus baselines. The code is available on GitHub, and the gains are reported with a common base model, so results depend on the chosen model and benchmarks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: LLM-based agents can retrieve materials data, operate simulation codes, and close design-synthesis-characterization loops with autonomous laboratories, but extending them with new capabilities, keeping long-running tasks on track, and letting them learn from past tasks remain difficult. The 'agent operating system' idea, as in AIOS, embeds the LLM in an OS-like layer that manages resources and tool access for agents. MatOS applies this idea to materials science by making data, tools, skills, and workflows first-class, reusable assets and by executing tasks as stateful graphs that can be resumed and traced.

## [AI reliance erodes skills, creating bistable adoption dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04886) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2611.04886v2) presents an analytical and simulation-based model showing that when agents imitate successful peers and their skills improve with practice but erode with AI reliance, AI adoption becomes bistable. The long-term outcome depends on the distribution of skills between AI users and non-users, not just average skill levels. This work highlights a potential societal risk: widespread AI adoption may be difficult to reverse because skill erosion creates a history-dependent switching cost. It suggests that policies promoting AI use should consider long-term skill distribution effects, not just immediate productivity gains. The model shows that a deterministic description tracking mean skills of both groups and the variance among non-users predicts outcomes as accurately as simulations, whereas means alone are insufficient. When AI benefits improve over time, adoption can jump abruptly, and a temporary benefit increase (e.g., low prices) can leave a population reliant on AI even after benefits revert.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Bistability means a system has two stable equilibrium states, like a light switch. Agent-based modeling simulates interactions of heterogeneous agents to study emergent social phenomena. Skill erosion refers to the decline in ability to perform tasks independently without AI support, a concept increasingly discussed in organizational contexts as distributed de-skilling.

## [LLMs Zero-Shot Weight-Space Program Ten Game Networks, Six Formally Verified](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09271) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.09271v3) investigates how far a large language model can perform zero-shot weight-space programming—setting every weight of a feed-forward network by hand from an explicit specification, with no gradient descent or training data—across a four-rung ladder of two-player games (Nim, tic-tac-toe, two sizes of Connect Four, and a chess endgame). Ten networks were built and independently verified, and six admit a complete, non-statistical proof of correctness against an independent oracle. This work pushes the boundary of what LLMs can do without training, showing they can directly author neural network weights for nontrivial game-playing tasks and that many of these hand-built networks are formally verifiable. It could open a new paradigm for program synthesis and AI safety, where model behavior is specified and proven rather than learned statistically. The chess-endgame rung is exact for cheap properties (move legality and, in one network, an exact one-ply mate detector) but collapses on expensive multi-move mating plans, dropping from 84.1–100% success on one-ply tactics to 0.57–9.13% in full adversarial play; a replicate shows the collapse is an order of magnitude less severe under a shorter required plan and exact safety checks. The standard-sized rung confirms the remaining gap is an architectural ceiling rather than an engineering issue, and the same collapse reproduces across two structurally unrelated games.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Weight-space programming is an emerging approach in which a general-purpose LLM reads, interprets, and directly writes the weights of another neural network, bypassing gradient descent and training loops entirely. Zero-shot learning refers to a model's ability to perform a task without any task-specific examples or fine-tuning. Formal verification provides mathematical proof that a system satisfies a specification, offering stronger guarantees than empirical testing. This paper combines these ideas to test whether LLMs can reliably generate verifiable neural networks for games of increasing complexity.

## [Lightfoot: GraphRAG with exact, model-free document deletion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11010) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Lightfoot is a new GraphRAG system that makes every graph element a deterministic function of per-passage extractions, enabling exact document deletion as a database transaction with no language-model calls. Deleting 10% of an 11,402-passage corpus takes 14 seconds and yields a graph identical to a fresh index of the surviving documents. This addresses a practical deployment cost that research comparisons often overlook: correctly removing documents from a graph index without expensive model calls or full rebuilds. It matters for production RAG systems that must handle data updates, deletions, and compliance requirements efficiently. On near-duplicate name corpora, HippoRAG 2's capped synonym lists leave 565 fewer edges than a rebuild, while Lightfoot remains exact. Under identical models, Lightfoot is competitive with HippoRAG 2, beating it on MuSiQue recall@5 and recall@10 (+4.8 and +5.5 points) and on 2WikiMultiHopQA recall@10 (+3.4), with answers tying on all three datasets; it also builds the cheapest index, 26-31% below HippoRAG 2.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: GraphRAG (Graph-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation) enhances multi-hop question answering by extracting entities and relationships from documents into a knowledge graph, then retrieving relevant subgraphs at query time. Unlike traditional RAG that uses vector embeddings of text chunks, GraphRAG captures structured relational information, but maintaining such graphs—especially deleting documents correctly—can be costly because it often requires language model calls or full rebuilds. HippoRAG 2 is a related GraphRAG approach that uses personalized PageRank over a graph with synonym edges to improve retrieval.

## [SkillEarth: Self-Evolving Skills Let General Agents Act on Earth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15481) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SkillEarth introduces a self-evolving skill extraction method that externalizes remote sensing tool-call knowledge into reusable, retrievable, and evolvable skill files, refined through observation-grounded evolution without expert intervention or parameter updates. Experiments on OpenEarth-Bench and Earth-Bench show improved tool selection, argument correctness, execution-order consistency, and task completion across three frontier and four open-source LLMs. This approach enables general-purpose agents to continually adapt to new remote sensing tools and tasks without retraining, addressing a key limitation of current RS agents that rely on predefined expertise or parameter updates. It could lower the barrier for deploying AI agents in dynamic Earth observation workflows and support cross-model reuse of skills. Unlike outcome-based reflection that refines skills holistically, SkillEarth grounds skill evolution in real tool-chain execution and combines dual-judge feedback with a grounded observation routing graph for component-level refinement. The resulting skills are generalizable, reliable, and transferable, improving performance across multiple LLMs without retraining.

## [LLM Hand-Writes Neural Network Weights from Data, No Gradients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19292) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.19292v2) tests whether a large language model can perform zero-shot weight-space programming: given a small supervised dataset and a feed-forward network architecture, the LLM writes out every weight and bias as a literal number without any optimizer, gradient, or code execution. Each construction is sealed and pre-registers its expected score, and the frozen network matches the closed-form optimum on linear problems, reaches irreducible error on three non-linear rungs, and beats every fitted baseline on two of those, including the same architecture trained by gradient descent. This work pushes the boundary of what LLMs can do without traditional training, suggesting a potential paradigm shift where models directly synthesize network parameters from data rather than relying on gradient-based optimization. If validated, it could enable rapid prototyping of small models and offer new insights into how LLMs perform statistical inference and structure discovery. The graded suite includes two toy problems, nine synthetic problems disguised by a hidden rotation, classical benchmarks, and one real engineering regression; the approach fails on two rungs where the structure is oblique to the analyzed coordinates, and three additional constructors reveal distinct failure modes, though one oblique-hinge failure is solved by another constructor with a margin surviving multiple-comparison correction. The training error of a linear separator predicts whether the hidden layer is useful, while a composite complexity index does not.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Weight-space programming is an emerging approach where a general-purpose LLM reads and directly writes the weights of another neural network, treating weights as a program to be edited, without gradient descent or a training loop. Previous demonstrations started from a target function given as a rule system, making it a compilation problem; this paper addresses the harder case where only a dataset is provided, requiring the LLM to perform statistical inference and express the result in parameter form.

A new arXiv paper (2505.16498v2) explores using Large Language Models to translate informal navigation instructions into Answer Set Programming rules, enabling autonomous vehicles to perform semantic navigation without predefined maps. The experimental evaluation shows that LLM-generated ASP constraints encode real-world urban driving logic and support explainable, semantic-based decision-making. This approach addresses a key limitation of current autonomous driving systems—their heavy reliance on predefined maps—by enabling real-time adaptability to dynamic environments such as road layout changes or missing map data. It also improves explainability by aligning machine reasoning with human communication of navigational intent, which could increase trust and safety in autonomous vehicles. The method leverages ASP's non-monotonic reasoning to allow vehicles to retract conclusions based on new evidence, adapting to evolving scenarios. However, the evaluation is limited to an experimental setting, and the paper does not yet demonstrate real-world deployment or scalability.

## [Continuous Skin Tone Modeling Improves Fairness in Skin Lesion Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11627) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2506.11627v2) proposes representing skin tone as a continuous attribute and assigning individual sample weights based on its nonlinear relationship with classification performance, rather than treating skin tone as a categorical attribute. Evaluations on two dermatological datasets show that fairness metrics are sensitive to skin-color categorization and that the proposed method yields more stable fairness improvements than conventional categorical reweighting while generally preserving classification performance. This work highlights a subtle but important flaw in fairness evaluation for medical imaging: treating skin tone as discrete categories can mask performance disparities across subtle tone variations. By offering a continuous, sample-level reweighting approach, it provides a more robust way to audit and mitigate bias in dermatology AI, which is critical as such models move toward clinical deployment and must serve diverse patient populations equitably. The method assigns weights to individual samples based on a nonlinear function of continuous skin tone, and experiments on two dermatological datasets show that fairness metrics vary depending on how skin color is categorized. The approach generally preserves classification performance, but the abstract does not specify the exact fairness metrics used or the magnitude of improvement over categorical reweighting.

MLBuf-RePlAce is introduced as the first open-source learning-driven virtual buffering-aware analytical global placement framework, built on OpenROAD. It uses recursive learning to predict buffer types and locations while addressing Electrical Rule Check (ERC) violations during global placement. This work addresses the growing interconnect delay dominance in advanced nodes by integrating buffer porosity awareness into placement, potentially improving timing closure in physical synthesis flows. As an open-source contribution to the EDA community, it provides a reproducible baseline for further research and adoption in both academic and commercial flows. MLBuf-RePlAce achieves maximum and average TNS improvements of 56% and 31% in the OpenROAD flow without post-route power degradation, and 53% and 28% in a commercial flow with only 0.2% average power increase. Evaluations use open-source testcases from TILOS MacroPlacement and OpenROAD-flow-scripts.

## [QUTCC: Quantile Uncertainty Training and Conformal Calibration for Imaging Inverse Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.14760) ⭐️ 7.0/10

QUTCC introduces a quantile regression and conformal calibration method to produce spatially adaptive uncertainty intervals for deep learning-based imaging inverse problems. It uses a U-Net with a quantile embedding to learn a full conditional quantile distribution, enabling non-linear, spatially-adaptive scaling during conformal calibration. This method addresses a key limitation of standard conformal prediction, which ignores spatial correlations in images and produces unnecessarily wide uncertainty intervals. By providing tighter, pixel-wise valid uncertainty estimates, QUTCC can improve reliability and trust in deep learning models for scientific and medical imaging, where failures and hallucinations can have serious consequences. QUTCC provides pixel-marginal coverage guarantees and can predict pixel-wise conditional probability density estimates without distributional assumptions. Evaluated on denoising, accelerated MRI, and quantitative phase microscopy, it consistently produces tighter intervals than prior conformal methods at the same coverage level and can help locate hallucinations in some cases.

**Background**: Conformal prediction is a framework for producing statistically valid uncertainty sets around predictions, but standard methods assume exchangeable data and ignore spatial dependencies in high-dimensional images. Imaging inverse problems, such as denoising or MRI reconstruction, involve recovering an image from noisy or incomplete measurements, and deep learning models are increasingly used for these tasks. Quantile regression is a technique that estimates conditional quantiles of a response variable, providing a way to model full distributions rather than just means.

## [Economic Model Reveals Non-Monotonic Openness and Policy Pitfalls for AI Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.15200) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2510.15200v2) presents a two-period game-theoretic model analyzing how an incumbent foundation model developer strategically chooses openness and licensing fees, showing that optimal openness can first decrease and then increase as usage-driven learning strengthens. It also finds that a full-openness mandate can backfire by inducing the incumbent to abandon a low-fee strategy, potentially creating an 'openness trap' that reduces welfare. The findings challenge the assumption that more openness is always better and show that well-intentioned policies like full-openness mandates or usage subsidies can have unintended consequences, which is crucial for ongoing AI governance and competition policy debates. The model also highlights how data flywheels and experience portability affect market dynamics, with implications for developers, deployers, and consumers. The model features an incumbent developer, a downstream deployer, and an entrant, where usage builds model-specific experience that lowers future adaptation costs, creating a data flywheel. Key results include conditions for non-monotonic openness, the possibility that a full-openness mandate reduces first-period adaptation and welfare, and that a small usage subsidy can paradoxically lead to higher fees and reduced openness, lowering welfare even before subsidy costs are considered.

**Background**: Foundation models like large language models are increasingly central to AI, and developers must decide how openly to share them—ranging from open weights to API access with fees. Openness lowers adaptation costs for downstream users but may help competitors, creating a strategic trade-off. This paper uses a two-period economic model to study how learning effects and competition shape these decisions, informing policy discussions on AI openness and governance.

## [MACE: MCTS-Guided Adversarial Co-Evolution for LLM Safety Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.19218) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce MACE, an MCTS-guided adversarial co-evolution framework that uses search statistics to train both attacker and defender policies for LLM safety alignment. Its GS-MCTS component explores multi-step compositions of rewriting operators, while AC-TGPO uses success frequency and path depth for difficulty-aware credit assignment and a temporally asymmetric curriculum. This work addresses the critical problem of defending against dynamic jailbreak attacks, which repeatedly reformulate harmful requests to bypass safety alignment. By connecting search structure to policy optimization and cross-round memory, MACE achieves state-of-the-art results, reducing average attack success rate from 11.9% to 8.8% compared to the best baseline. Ablation studies show that removing difficulty-aware weighting, asymmetric samples, or persistent hard samples increases defense ASR-LR by 3.7%, 6.0%, and 5.0%, respectively. The attacker achieves the highest ASR-LR across all six model-dataset settings, demonstrating the effectiveness of the co-evolutionary approach.

**Background**: Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) is a heuristic search algorithm that builds a search tree using random simulations, commonly used in decision processes like board games. Jailbreak attacks manipulate LLMs into generating harmful responses by designing adversarial prompts that bypass safety guardrails. Adversarial co-evolution trains attacker and defender models together, allowing each to adapt to the other's strategies over time.

A new arXiv paper (2603.17673v3) proposes a two-stage post-training recipe that turns a small 4B local language model into a security agent for Linux privilege escalation. Researchers introduced WHBench, a benchmark of 47 expert-crafted scenarios across 10 women's health topics, evaluated on 22 models using a 23-criterion rubric. It provides a public tool to track progress toward safer and more equitable AI in clinical settings. WHBench uses safety-weighted penalties and server-side score recalculation, with moderate inter-rater reliability at the response label level but high reliability for model ranking. The rubric covers clinical accuracy, completeness, safety, communication quality, instruction following, equity, uncertainty handling, and guideline adherence.

## [VLMs Over-Correct Student Math Errors, New Metric PINK Penalizes It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.22774) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper presents the first systematic study of multi-line handwritten math OCR, revealing that Vision-Language Models (VLMs) often 'fix' student errors instead of transcribing them faithfully. The authors propose PINK (Penalized INK-based score), a semantic evaluation metric that uses an LLM for rubric-based grading and explicitly penalizes over-correction. Over-correction hides the very mistakes that educational assessment aims to detect, undermining the reliability of AI-powered grading and tutoring systems. PINK aligns significantly better with human expert judgment (55.0% preference vs. BLEU's 39.5%) and reveals substantial ranking reversals among 15 state-of-the-art VLMs, suggesting current benchmarks mislead model selection for educational OCR. PINK uses a two-stage LLM-based auto-grader with a five-dimension rubric and 0–20 partial credit, penalizing cases where the transcription appears better than the student's original work. Evaluation on the FERMAT dataset shows GPT-4o is heavily penalized for aggressive over-correction, while Gemini 2.5 Flash emerges as the most faithful transcriber.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Handwritten math OCR is important for educational AI systems that automatically grade or tutor students, but most prior benchmarks focus on single-line expressions and use lexical metrics like BLEU. BLEU measures surface-level n-gram overlap and cannot assess semantic reasoning across multi-line solutions, making it ill-suited for detecting whether a model preserved or altered a student's mistakes. Vision-Language Models (VLMs) are AI models that process both images and text, and they are increasingly used for document and handwriting recognition.

## [CyBiasBench: Benchmarking Bias in LLM Agents for Cyber-Attack Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07830) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced CyBiasBench, a 630-session benchmark that evaluates five LLM agents across three targets, four prompt conditions, and ten attack families, revealing distinct and prompt-stable attack-selection biases. The study also identifies a 'bias momentum' effect where agents resist explicit steering toward attack families that conflict with their inherent bias, without measurable performance gains. This benchmark provides a systematic way to quantify behavioral biases in LLM agents beyond aggregate success rates, which is crucial for AI safety and red-team evaluation in offensive cybersecurity. It highlights that agent behavior is not easily steerable, raising concerns for both defensive monitoring and responsible deployment of autonomous cyber agents. The benchmark uses ten attack families and measures entropy in attack-family allocation distributions, showing each agent has a different dominant family and varying concentration. The authors release an interactive dashboard and a reproducibility artifact with session-level statistics and evaluation scripts, though the study is limited to five agents and three targets.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: LLM agents are increasingly used in offensive cybersecurity for tasks like penetration testing and red teaming, where they autonomously select and execute attack strategies. Prior work has shown that LLMs can exhibit biases in tool selection and decision-making, but attack-selection bias in cyber contexts had not been systematically quantified. CyBiasBench fills this gap by measuring how agents distribute effort across attack families and how stable these patterns are under prompt variations.

## [iMuon extends Muon optimizer to Riemannian matrix manifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09238) ⭐️ 7.0/10

iMuon introduces an intrinsic spectral optimization method that generalizes Muon's linear maximization oracle (LMO) to Riemannian matrix manifolds by lifting unitarily invariant norms, preserving quotient symmetries and enabling closed-form solutions. The framework provides closed-form updates on fixed-rank, SPD, Stiefel, and Grassmann manifolds for spectral, Frobenius, and nuclear norms, with convergence rates depending only on manifold dimension. This work bridges norm-constrained optimization with Riemannian geometry, enabling Muon-style optimizers to be applied to manifold-valued parameters such as low-rank factorizations, orthogonality constraints, and SPD matrices. It could impact large-scale learning, particularly LoRA fine-tuning of LLMs and subspace learning, by providing theoretically grounded and efficient updates. The intrinsic LMO is symmetry-preserving and reduces to a standard Euclidean LMO under a structural condition on the scaled tangent space. On the fixed-rank manifold, the convergence constant depends only on the rank, making the rate independent of factor conditioning and removing the runtime factor rescaling required by prior work.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Muon is a recently proposed optimizer that computes updates via a linear maximization oracle over a matrix-norm ball in Euclidean space, and it has become central to large-scale learning. Riemannian optimization generalizes Euclidean optimization techniques to manifolds, which are spaces that locally resemble Euclidean space but have global curvature, such as the set of orthogonal matrices or symmetric positive-definite matrices. Unitarily invariant norms are matrix norms that are invariant under unitary transformations, and they include the spectral, Frobenius, and nuclear norms.

## [SkyPart: Weather-Robust Cross-View Geo-Localization via Prototype-Based Semantic Part Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11654) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SkyPart introduces a lightweight, swappable head for patch-based vision transformers that explicitly groups patches into semantic parts using learnable prototypes, altitude-conditioned modulation, graph-attention readout, and uncertainty-weighted multi-objective learning. It achieves state-of-the-art results on SUES-200, University-1652, and DenseUAV with only 26.95M parameters and 22.14 GFLOPs, and its accuracy advantage grows under ten WeatherPrompt corruption conditions. This work addresses key limitations in cross-view geo-localization for drone navigation, offering a more robust and efficient solution for GNSS-denied environments. Its lightweight design and strong performance under weather corruptions make it practical for real-world deployment in delivery, inspection, and search-and-rescue missions. SkyPart uses single-pass retrieval without reranking or test-time augmentation, and its altitude-conditioned linear modulation is applied only during training to yield altitude-free embeddings at inference. The method is the smallest among top-performing approaches in the comparison, and code is available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: Cross-view geo-localization (CVGL) matches an oblique drone view to a geo-referenced satellite tile, providing an alternative to GNSS for autonomous localization when satellite signals are jammed or unavailable. Vision transformers (ViTs) process images as sequences of patches, and recent CVGL methods often compress patch grids into global descriptors, which can lose spatial layout information. SkyPart builds on these concepts by introducing explicit part grouping over the patch grid to better separate view-shared layout from view-specific texture.

## [Generative Retrieval Boosts E-commerce Search with Semantic Cluster IDs and Expert-Guided RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14434) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes CQ-SID and EG-GRPO, a generative retrieval framework that uses category-and-query constrained semantic cluster IDs and expert-guided reinforcement learning as a recall-stage supplement for industrial e-commerce search. Offline experiments on TmallAPP search logs show up to 26.76% and 11.11% relative gains in semantic and personalized click hitrate over RQ-VAE baselines while halving beam search size, and online A/B tests confirm GMV +1.15% and UCTCVR +0.40%. This work demonstrates a practical path for deploying generative retrieval in real-world e-commerce systems, where massive dynamic catalogs and strict latency requirements have hindered adoption. The generative recall channel now contributes over 50.25% of exposures, 58.96% of clicks, and 72.63% of purchases in production, showing significant business impact. CQ-SID employs category-aware and query-item contrastive learning along with Residual Quantized VAEs to encode items into hierarchical semantic cluster identifiers, reducing beam search complexity. EG-GRPO aligns generative recall with downstream ranking under sparse rewards by injecting ground-truth samples to stabilize training.

## [Distributional Alignment Metric Guides Task Vector Design for In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20730) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces d_NTP, a metric measuring the discrepancy in next-token probabilities between task vector-based and in-context learning (ICL) inference, and shows it strongly negatively correlates with downstream accuracy. Motivated by this, the authors propose Linear Task Vector (LTV), a closed-form linear mapping that minimizes d_NTP and improves average accuracy by 9.2% across eight classification benchmarks and five LLMs. This work provides a principled proxy for evaluating task vectors beyond downstream accuracy, enabling more efficient design of task vector extraction methods. It could significantly reduce inference costs for LLMs by compressing demonstrations into compact representations without sacrificing performance. LTV uses a closed-form linear mapping via regression to estimate demonstration effects, reducing inference latency. The authors also show that task vectors from a larger model can enhance a smaller model's performance by 6.4%, demonstrating cross-scale transferability.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: In-context learning (ICL) allows LLMs to adapt to new tasks by providing demonstrations in the prompt, but inference costs grow with context length. Task vectors compress these demonstrations into hidden-state representations, but their quality has been evaluated only indirectly via downstream accuracy. This paper proposes distributional alignment as a more direct criterion, measuring how well the task vector's predictive distribution matches that of full ICL.

## [Latent Q-Barrier Shield Boosts Safety in In-Context RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25267) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces a latent Q-Barrier shield for safe in-context reinforcement learning (ICRL) that, without parameter updates, infers context from interaction history and filters or reweights candidate actions using the remaining safety budget and predicted future cost. It provides a theoretical conditional one-step barrier-margin bound and shows improved reward-safety tradeoffs over a strong safe-ICRL baseline on five benchmarks. This work addresses a critical gap in safe ICRL: under out-of-distribution deployment shifts, pretraining-only safety mechanisms may fail to enforce cost constraints because the budget only influences behavior through frozen policy conditioning. The proposed action-level shield could enable more reliable safety guarantees in real-world deployments where test-time parameter updates are infeasible. The shield learns a context representation, latent dynamics model, and an ensemble cost critic before deployment, then uses these to predict future costs and enforce safety without any gradient updates. The theoretical bound includes explicit Bellman and latent-prediction error terms, and experiments show higher return in four of five benchmarks while matching or lowering average episode cost in all five.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 18, 05:00

**Background**: In-context reinforcement learning (ICRL) is a paradigm where an agent, after pretraining, adapts to new tasks at test time purely from interaction history without updating its parameters. Safe ICRL extends this by requiring the agent to respect a cost budget during episodes. A shield is a runtime safety mechanism that monitors and modifies actions to prevent unsafe outcomes, and here it operates in a learned latent space using a Q-barrier function to estimate future costs.
